## Preface.

### Gentle Reader,

Man has received nothing more excellent from GOD than his intellect <note>The original German "Verstand" refers to the faculty of understanding or reason</note>: for as soon as he is disturbed in the same, he immediately becomes either a child, or worse than a wild beast, and is thus unfit to honor God and to serve mankind. In such a manner, one can be called a human being all the more, the more he knows how to use the powers of his intellect. And therefore everyone, but especially he who wishes to be or become a scholar <term>Gelehrter: a person of learning or a member of the academic class</term>, should strive with right zeal toward how he might attain such a quickened spir—

<summary>This preface introduces the work by arguing that human reason is the highest gift from God, distinguishing humans from animals and enabling them to serve society.</summary>
<keywords>intellect, reason, divinity, scholarship, human nature, preface</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface, likely following a discussion on the purpose of education or the nature of the mind. The catchword "te" suggests the next page begins with a word like "teils" or "terie."</meta>

# Preface

might reach the use of the powers of his understanding <note>German: Verstand; refers to the faculty of reason and logical thought</note> as much as is humanly possible. However, very few people think of this, and most turn scholarship into a mere work of memory, fleeing from reflection <note>German: Nachsinnen; deep, meditative thought</note> more than they would from a snake. Therefore, everything is hateful to them that requires reflection and brings them to a ready use of their understanding; on the other hand, they find pleasant whatever they can grasp like a fairy tale while half-asleep, leaving them with the same way of thinking they have shared with unlearned people since childhood. One cannot, however, recognize the powers of the human understanding in any other way than through experience, by using them. In such a manner, those who only compile the thoughts of others, having never practiced the art of invention <note>German: Erfinden; in this context, the ability to discover new ideas or logical proofs</note>, nor having grasped a single demonstrated—that is, right thoroughly executed—truth in their entire lives, can know little or nothing of the powers of the understanding and their use, unless they have read something about it in suitable books. But books in this mat-

<summary>The author critiques the common educational practice of relying on rote memory rather than active reflection, arguing that true understanding only comes through the practical experience of reasoning.</summary>
<keywords>understanding, scholarship, memory, reflection, experience, invention, logic, pedagogy</keywords>

-># Preface<-

[the]matics, people write either according to a completely different method than is common in that field, or they busy themselves with applying the **mathematical method**. In the first case, sensible people see a very great confusion; for they soon complain that words are not at all, or only rarely, adequately explained, many things are assumed without proof, and others are not sufficiently—and often even incorrectly—proven. In the second case, however, no one has yet succeeded who wanted to present **mathematical demonstrations** <term>demonstrations; from the Latin 'demonstratio,' referring to a logical proof that shows a conclusion must necessarily follow from its premises</term> in disciplines other than mathematics. *Descartes*, although he was a great *mathematician* <note>original Latin: *Mathematicus*; René Descartes was a pioneer in both philosophy and analytical geometry</note>, never showed the weakness of his proof that there is a **GOD** more clearly than when he was persuaded to present it in a **geometrical manner**. *Spinoza* in his *Ethics* <note>original Latin: *Ethica*</note> and *Raphson* <note>Joseph Raphson (c. 1648–1715), an English mathematician known for the Newton-Raphson method</note> in his *Demonstration of God* <note>original Latin: *Demonstratione de Deo*</note> conducted themselves very poorly in the act of demonstrating, even though both did not lack intellect and both were not unexperienced in mathematics. They explain many words through others equal— <meta>catchword: equal- (German: gleich=); the sentence likely continues with "equally obscure" or "equivalent words" on the next page.</meta>

<summary>The author critiques how scholars attempt to use mathematical rigor in other fields, arguing that even great thinkers like Descartes and Spinoza failed when trying to prove the existence of God or ethical truths using geometric-style proofs.</summary>
<keywords>mathematical method, proof of God, Descartes, Spinoza, Raphson, demonstration, logic, ethics, geometry</keywords>

-># Preface<-

)( 4

indifferent [words], and take for granted without proof that which often should have been proven most of all; indeed, they sometimes do not even connect their conclusions with one another, as should rightly be done. For this reason, only the **mathematical sciences** <term>mathematical sciences; in the 18th century, this term encompassed not just arithmetic and geometry, but also "applied" fields like mechanics, optics, and even music theory</term> remain, from which one can see the correct use of the **powers of the understanding**. And with this intention, in my *Principles of the Mathematical Sciences* <note>original German: *Anfangs-Gründen der Mathematischen Wissenschafften*; this refers to Christian Wolff’s foundational textbook which aimed to organize all knowledge according to mathematical rigor</note>, I have endeavored to present everything in such a manner, as far as possible, as it could have been discovered; and I also take care in my **lectures** <note>original: *Collegiis*; from the Latin *collegium*, referring to university courses or lectures</note> to incorporate such observations, so that, as much as is in my power, I may lead my listeners to reflection, since otherwise many obstacles are placed in their way.

However, no one should apply the common proverb to me, *every shepherd praises his staff* <note>original German: *ein jeder Schäfer lobet seine Keute*; a folk idiom similar to "every potter praises his own pot," meaning everyone speaks well of their own profession or tools</note>: for whoever knows me and does not wish to speak against his conscience will not refrain from giving me the **testimony** that the **love for truth** is above all else for me, and no one should dare to [place] me among the

->### Preface ###<-

to say to my face <note>The text continues from the previous page: "no one should dare to [say to my] face..."</note> that I speak, much less write, out of a **self-interested mind** <term>self-interested; in the 18th century, "interest" often referred to personal financial gain or biased motives that clouded objective truth</term>. Rather, I often refrain from speaking the truth and would rather forgo the profit I might rightfully have, whenever I see it could give cause to consider me self-interested. Therefore, I would also rather cite the **testimony of others** here than appeal to my own experience.

In our parts, **Locke's** work on the **human understanding** <note>original: *Lockens Werck vom Verstande des Menschen*; referring to John Locke’s "An Essay Concerning Human Understanding" (1689)</note> is generally held to be exceedingly ingenious, so that even those who contemptuously despise all his countrymen nevertheless exalt him. But, dear reader, to what does Locke attribute the ability of his understanding, and what does he recommend as a means to become **sharp-witted**? 

One should look among the works that were published in London in 1706 after his death, in the treatise *Of the Conduct of the Understanding* <note>original: *Leitung des menschlichen Verstandes*</note>, p. 32 and following <note>original: *& seqq.*; Latin abbreviation for *et sequentes*, meaning "and the following pages"</note>; there one will find that he attributes his sharpness of mind to **mathematics**, and makes even more of a boast of mathematics, especially **algebra**, as a means to right

**right use of the understanding** to attain, which I—due to deeply rooted prejudices and passions—was unwilling to do for many of them. I do not wish to repeat other testimonies here, because they have already been cited in the preface concerning the instruction of the **mathematical method** in my *Principles of the Mathematical Sciences* <note>original: *Anfangs-Gründen der Mathematischen Wissenschafften*; this refers to Christian Wolff’s foundational multi-volume work on mathematics and physics, first published in 1710</note>. And this has been precisely one of my main intentions why I applied myself seriously to **mathematics**: not that I learned it as a **trade** to earn my bread; for it was likely never my true intention to serve as a **professor of mathematics** <term>Professor Matheseos; "Matheseos" is the Latin genitive form of "Mathesis," meaning "of mathematics"</term>, until I received my first **calling** <term>vocation; from the Latin *vocatio*, referring to a formal appointment to a university chair or office</term> to it, which I regarded as a divine sign. What I have now noted through the reading of mathematical writings and through my own manifold **reflection** on the use of the powers of the human understanding, I have wished to compose into short rules—specifically the easiest points and those most necessary to know first. For if I were to write everything that I [have found] through my

**reflection** learned to recognize; I would serve few of my fellow Germans, as to most of them it would soon seem like **whims** <note>original: *Grillen*; literally "crickets," a common 18th-century idiom for idle fancies, melancholy, or capricious thoughts that distract the mind</note> if they were required to keep their thoughts together for a bit too long; and it would also not be suitable for **beginners**, for whose sake I have entrusted the present thoughts to the press. And this is precisely one of the reasons why I have written it in **German**, whereas among foreigners there are many who place great value on thorough knowledge: for their sake, on another occasion, something more complete on this matter shall be shared in the **Latin language**, just as I allowed more **theoretical matters** <term>theoretica; referring to the abstract or speculative principles of a science as opposed to its practical application</term> to flow into the Latin edition of my **Principles of Mathematics** <term>Elementorum Matheseos; the Latin title of Wolff’s foundational work *Elements of Mathematics*</term> than was permitted to enter the German one. ->### Preface ###<-

**will find.** However, I cannot hide the fact that it will greatly contribute to the **ready use** of these rules if one diligently reviews even my *Foundational Principles of the Mathematical Sciences* <term>Anfangs-Gründe der Mathematischen Wissenschafften; Wolff’s influential textbook that applied mathematical rigor to various fields of knowledge</term> and observes how the rules are applied there. For **skill** <term>Fertigkeit; the facility or "dexterity" of the mind to perform a task quickly and correctly</term> comes from **practice**; and practice consists of frequently reflecting on things that have been correctly proven. Even if the best **fencing master** <note>Fencing was a common metaphor in 18th-century logic to illustrate that theoretical knowledge (maxims) is useless without physical or mental "drilling."</note> were to explain the most excellent maxims of swordplay, one would still not be able to fence well if they had never actually picked up a sword. Much less would one learn to fence if the person attempting to teach the rules had never himself participated in a match.

To be sure, among us Germans today, youth are rarely encouraged toward **thorough sciences**, especially in those places where "teachers of ignorance" <note>original: *Lehrer der Unwissenheit*; a sharp critique of educators who preferred rote memorization or superficial rhetoric over the rigorous "mathematical method" Wolff championed</note> are gaining the upper hand. In such places, it is passed off as a special form of wisdom when **study** is turned into a mere **trade** <term>Handwercke; literally a "handicraft," suggesting a mechanical, unthinking job or a way to earn a living rather than a pursuit of truth</term>; yet, because through this

by unskilled tradesmen, but rather by merely self-conceited bunglers, yet even those who are now unable to see in their blindness will eventually have their eyes opened. Let one study something thorough in mathematics and philosophy <term>Welt-Weißheit; literally "world-wisdom," the standard 18th-century German term for philosophy, encompassing logic, ethics, and natural sciences</term> when he arrives at the university; only then will he realize how the decrees <term>Rescripte; official written orders or edicts issued by a sovereign</term> issued by our most gracious King and Lord out of his paternal concern for the land (a) emphasize it.

---

(a) The content of one of the Royal Decrees is this: The professors shall make helpful representations to each and every person residing at our University of Halle <note>The University of Halle was a center of the German Enlightenment and the primary site of Wolff's career and eventual exile.</note> who studies at their own expense, whoever they may be, that they should apply themselves during the first year primarily to philosophical studies <note>original: *studia Philosophica*</note> and more refined literature <note>original: *elegantiorem litteraturam*; referring to the humanities, classical languages, and rhetoric</note>, but afterward, once they have laid good foundations <note>original: *fundamenta*</note>, they may then proceed to the higher faculties <note>original: *ad superiores Facultates*; in the traditional university hierarchy, these were the professional schools of Theology, Law, and Medicine</note>. However, those who [receive] our own [stipends] or in our—

# Reflection
## Regarding the second edition.

It was first deemed useful 
to add several 
examples from all 
types of sciences <term>Wissenschafften; in the 18th century, this term encompassed all branches of academic knowledge, including the humanities, law, and natural philosophy</term> 
for the explanation of the 
rules, so that 
those who do 
not hear the explanation from me in person <note>Wolff is referring to students who attended his live lectures at the university</note> might 
find their way through it all the 
more easily: as 
it is known to everyone that 
examples do not only serve 
to better understand the rules, 
but also show how 
one should apply them in the 
proper place. 
This was done especially 
in the first chapter 
regarding concepts <term>Begriffen; the mental representations or "ideas" that form the most basic unit of logic in Wolff’s system</term>, 
because this is a primary subject <term>Haupt-Materie; the fundamental core material</term> 
upon which much 
depends, and which 
one does not find so 
fully developed in other 
books. 
And I have the hope 
that everyone will 
see from this that my 
rules—

rules are universal, and do not only apply in
mathematics, but everywhere
where one strives for a thorough
knowledge <term>Erkäntniß; the certain and grounded understanding of a subject</term>. Because I show in the chap-
ter on inferences <term>Schlüssen; logical deductions or syllogisms where a conclusion follows from premises</term> that one must make use
of formal inferences in the process of discovery <term>Erfinden; the methodical search for new truths or solutions</term>
if one wishes to think orderly
and proceed without missteps; so I
have deemed it not unhelpful that it
should come immediately after the chapter on propositions <term>Sätzen; logical statements or sentences that affirm or deny something</term>,

is used in the subsequent chap-

profitable to them <note>Wolff is likely critiquing "popular philosophers" or rhetoricians who preferred persuasive speech over the difficult, rigorous work of formal logic</note>

<summary>Wolff argues that logical rules are universal across all sciences and explains his decision to place the chapter on formal inferences early in the book to provide a foundation for discovery and proof. He defends his rigorous method against critics who prioritize social reputation over the difficult pursuit of clear concepts.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the preface of Christian Wolff’s work on logic. The catchword "ist" (is) from the previous page begins the first sentence here.</meta>

is than a well-founded reputation among the discerning. However, since for me truth stands above all else, I can well leave them <note>The "them" refers to the critics mentioned on the previous page who reject formal logic</note> to their five senses; but I shall never, out of self-interested motives, abandon thorough knowledge <term>Erkäntniß; the certain and grounded understanding of a subject</term>. Whoever wishes to use the rules of distinct concepts and orderly proofs <term>Beweise; rigorous demonstrations that establish the truth of a statement through logical steps</term> without stumbling must, as I have often reminded, study mathematics alongside them. In this way, these rules will become clear and easy for him, and he will no longer be tossed to and fro by every wind of doctrine <note>A reference to the biblical verse Ephesians 4:14, suggesting that without logic, one is easily swayed by deceptive arguments</note>, as we see happening to those who wish to use their five senses instead of the intellect <term>Verstand; the faculty of the mind that seeks truth through reasoning rather than just sensory perception</term>.

-> Halle, March 5, 1719. <-

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the "Notice Regarding the Third Edition" of Christian Wolff’s treatise on logic. It follows the previous prefatory material, establishing the author’s personal commitment to the rigorous methods he describes.</meta>

# Notice.
## Regarding the third edition.

What is written in this little book, I have always counted among the best of what I know: for I have many times experienced the benefit of the rules provided herein for the investigation and evaluation of the sciences <term>Wissenschafften; in the 18th century, this term referred to any systematic body of knowledge, including philosophy and logic, not just the natural sciences</term>, even the mathematical ones, and I still experience it daily while I am occupied with them. I can also say that I have devoted more time to no subject than to the primary matters that I have presented in this book and which are the foundation of the rest; and finally, after many detours and much consideration, I have found and established what I propose herein. However, just as I, for my own part, have concerned myself with these important truths regarding the guidance of the intellect <term>Leitung des Verstandes; the disciplined direction of the mind's reasoning faculties</term> in the pursuit of the knowledge <term>Erkäntnis; the certain grasp of truth through reason</term> of the truth, solely for the purpose that I might be placed in a position to recognize the truth thoroughly and with certainty <term>Gewisheit; the philosophical goal of reaching knowledge that is beyond doubt through logical proof</term> and to others... <note>original: "und anderen"; the sentence breaks off here at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>Christian Wolff introduces the third edition of his work, asserting that the logical rules it contains are the result of years of reflection and are essential for certain knowledge in all sciences. He characterizes these rules as the foundation of his own intellectual practice and a necessary guide for others seeking truth.</summary>

<keywords>logic, third edition, intellectual guidance, certainty, mathematical method, Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, where Wolff explains his motivation for publishing his logical rules to help others navigate the path to truth.</meta>

(o)

b 4

...to pave a smooth path for them, upon which they might reach useful knowledge <term>Erkäntnis; in Enlightenment philosophy, the certain grasp of truth through the disciplined use of reason</term> more securely and without stumbling or taking detours. It was with this intention that I made them known, so that others might likewise enjoy the benefit which I myself enjoy in abundance. I am therefore not a little pleased to learn, from the frequent sales <note>original: "häuffigen Abgange"; referring to the book’s commercial success and wide circulation</note> of this little book and from many reports received from all sorts of places, how others perceive the utility of my rules and are beginning to value them as much as I do. However, they will be able to use these rules with as much success as I have, if they have the time and opportunity to practice mathematical demonstrations <term>mathematische Demonstrationen; the application of rigorous, step-by-step logical proofs to ensure a conclusion is undeniable</term>, or, should circumstances not allow for that, if they diligently examine the writings on philosophy <term>Welt-Weisheit; literally "world-wisdom," the standard 18th-century German term for the study of philosophy and the nature of the universe</term> that I have published according to these rules. To make this easier for them to accomplish, I have sought to provide some guidance in this new edition. To this end, the fundamental rules <term>Grund-Regeln; the core principles of logic that form the basis of Wolff's system</term> have been explained with several more examples, so that they may be better understood, and I have also indicated therein,

where one finds subjects in my **writings** <note>original: "Schrifften"</note> by which these **rules** <note>original: "Regeln"</note> are illustrated. And this was the sole reason why I considered some further additions for this **third edition** <note>original: "Dritten Auflage"</note>. It is, to be sure, only a small amount that has been added here and there; however, this little is to be valued more highly than the small amount of money one usually pays for such a small book. The division of the chapters, and of these into their sections <note>original: "§§."</note>, has remained exactly as in the previous edition, for the very same reason that I made no changes in this respect in the new edition of my **Thoughts on God, the World, and the Soul of Man** <term>Gedancken von Gott, der Welt u. der Seele des Menschen; Christian Wolff’s foundational work on metaphysics (1719), which applied his logical method to the most fundamental questions of existence</term>. Just as I initially included nothing in this little book but that which is of certain use and which a person needs to keep for their entire life if they wish to progress successfully in the **sciences** <term>Wissenschafften; in the 18th century, this term encompassed all branches of systematic knowledge and academic study, including philosophy, law, and the natural sciences</term>; so I also find no reason why I should change my opinion now. For regardless of the fact that much more could have been included, which also has its utility, but is not so indispensable for everyone

<summary>Wolff explains that the third edition contains new cross-references to his work on metaphysics to illustrate logical rules, while maintaining the original chapter structure for consistency. He emphasizes that he has only included essential knowledge necessary for lifelong success in any academic discipline.</summary>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page concludes Christian Wolff's preface to the third edition of his logic manual. He justifies the brevity of the work by arguing that practical application of a few core principles is superior to an overwhelming list of theoretical rules.</meta>

(0)
<sig>b 5</sig>

how that which I have presented; yet it is not suited to the present undertaking, and I would have thereby made the little book useless for many. In the future, opportunity will arise where I will further elaborate on these matters for those who are insatiable for **rules** <note>original: "Regeln"</note>, and I shall expand them with much that belongs to those who wish to go further than is commonly done. For now, I content myself with that which is most necessary, especially since, regardless of that, in the **attainment of truth** <term>Erkäntnis der Wahrheit; the process of acquiring certain knowledge through the correct application of the intellect</term>, it does not depend on many rules, but rather on the frequent use of a few rules. **Practice** <note>original: "Ubung"</note> must do more than rules. He who learns to use a few rules correctly can, after this, find more for himself when he has need of them. Whoever studies **algebra** <term>Algebra; in Wolff's time, this was seen not just as math, but as a method for solving problems by substituting symbols for unknown values, serving as a model for logical precision</term> in such a way as I am accustomed to presenting it, will immediately give full credence to my words; and others will, in time, be convinced through experience that I speak the truth: which I heartily wish for everyone who thirsts for truth.

Halle,
->February 10, 1722.<-

<summary>Wolff concludes his preface by emphasizing that mastery of logic comes from the frequent practice of a few essential rules rather than exhaustive theory, citing algebra as a model for this disciplined way of thinking.</summary>

# Contents of the Entire Work.

### Preliminary Discourse.

§. 1. What **philosophy** <term>Welt-Weisheit; literally "world-wisdom," this was the standard German term for philosophy in the 18th century</term> is.
§. 2. What **science** <term>Wissenschafft; for Wolff, this refers to the ability to prove everything one asserts from undeniable grounds</term> is.
§. 3. What one calls "possible."
§. 4. That everything has a **ground** <note>original: "Grund"; refers to the Principle of Sufficient Reason—the idea that nothing exists without a reason why it is so and not otherwise</note> why it is.
§. 5. The nature of a philosopher's **knowledge** <note>original: "Erkäntniß"</note>.
§. 6. How it is distinguished from the knowledge of common people.
§. 7. What benefit the knowledge of a philosopher brings.
§. 8. What objection can be made against the definition of philosophy.
§. 9. How it is answered.
§. 10. What the first part of philosophy, or **Logic**, is.
§. 11. What the second part, or **natural theology** <note>The study of God based on reason and nature rather than revelation</note>, is.
§. 12. What the third part, or **pneumatics** <term>Pnevmatick; the branch of philosophy concerning spirits, the soul, and non-material substances</term> and **physics**, is.
§. 13. What the fourth part, or **ethics** and **politics**, is.
§. 14. What the fifth part, or **ontology** <term>Ontologie; the study of being or existence in general</term>, as well as **metaphysics**, is.
§. 15. What **mathematics** has to do with philosophy.
§. 16. What benefit mathematics creates therein.

<summary>This page provides the table of contents for the "Preliminary Discourse," outlining Christian Wolff’s systematic approach to defining philosophy, science, and the various branches of knowledge including logic, metaphysics, and physics.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophy, Science, Principle of Sufficient Reason, Logic, Natural Theology, Pneumatics, Ontology, Metaphysics, Mathematics</keywords>

### 17. The purpose of the present work is
### explained.

## Chapter 1.

# On the Concepts of
# Things.

§. 1. What is called **sensation** <term>empfinden; the basic act of perceiving through the senses</term>.
2. What is called a **thought** <term>Gedancke</term>.
3. What are called the **senses** <term>Sinnen</term>.
4. What is understood by a **concept** <term>Begriff; the mental representation of an object</term>.
5. That the senses lead us to a concept.
6. How this happens must not and cannot be investigated here <note>This likely refers to the fact that the physiological or metaphysical process of how senses become thoughts belongs to "Psychology" or "Pneumatics," not Logic</note>.
7. What we must do when the senses are to prompt us toward a concept.
8. When this is easy or difficult to put into practice.
9. How **clear** and **obscure** <term>dunckele; literally "dark" or "dim"</term> concepts are distinguished from one another.
10. That obscure concepts have their degrees.
11. Examples of obscure concepts are provided.
12. What precautions one must take regarding obscure concepts.
13. How a **distinct** <term>deutlich</term> concept is distinguished from an **indistinct** one.
14. How each of these may be conveyed to another person.
15. How a **detailed** <term>ausführlich</term> concept is distinguished from one that is not detailed.
16. How a **complete** <term>vollständig</term> concept is distinguished from an incomplete one.
17. That complete concepts have their degrees.
18. To what extent one must **analyze** <term>zergliedern; literally "to take apart limb by limb"</term> distinct concepts.
19. How a distinct concept is obtained.

### Chapter 2.
# On the Use of Words.

§. 1. What words are.
2. When two persons understand one another.
3. That every word must have its **precisely measured meaning** <note>abgemessene Bedeutung; implying that for clear communication, a word's definition must be fixed and not vague</note>.
4. How one learns whether they understand what they are saying.
5. That in speaking, one does not always think of the meaning of the words.
6. 7. That empty words can be understandable.
8. How words and things are to be distinguished from one another.
9. That one must distinguish the concept of the **sound** <term>Thones; the physical noise or phonetic quality of a word</term> of the words from the concept of the thing that it signifies.

10. That one can speak empty words with one another and yet understand each other.

### Chapter 3.
## On Propositions.

<term>Sätzen; in this context, "propositions" refers to statements or sentences that affirm or deny something and can be judged as true or false</term>

§. 1. When we **judge** <term>urtheilen; the mental process of forming an opinion or making a logical decision about the truth of a matter</term>.
2. In what judging consists.
3. What a **proposition** is, and how one through which something is affirmed is distinguished from that through which something is denied.
4. What is required if one is to understand a proposition correctly.
5. How general propositions are distinguished from particular ones, and what basis both have.
6. How one should **analyze** <term>zergliedern; literally "to dismember," meaning to break a complex statement down into its logical components</term> propositions.
7. An **objection** <term>Einwurff; a counter-argument</term> is made against this and answered.
8. What use the analysis of propositions has.
9. The internal nature of propositions is explained.
10. When we can **conceive** <term>gedencken; here meaning to form a mental representation or to think of something as possible</term> of a proposition, or cannot conceive of it, or do not know whether it allows itself to be conceived or not.

## Preliminary Report.
### Concerning
# Philosophy.

->§. 1.<-

<margin>What philosophy is.</margin>
Philosophy <note>original: "Welt-Weißheit"; literally "world-wisdom." In the 18th century, German thinkers used this term to describe philosophy as a practical and comprehensive understanding of the world.</note> is a science of all possible things—how and why they are possible.

->§. 2.<-

<margin>What science is.</margin>
By science <term>Wissenschafft; in this historical context, this refers to a systematic discipline or a rigorous body of knowledge based on logical proof, rather than just the modern "natural sciences."</term> I understand a skill of the intellect <term>Verstand; the faculty of the mind responsible for reasoning and logical processing.</term> to irrefutably demonstrate everything that one asserts, based on grounds that cannot be contradicted. Which grounds are uncontradictable, and how one demonstrates something in an irrefutable manner, will be demonstrated in these present thoughts regarding the use of the powers of the intellect in the pursuit of truth.

§. 4. Since nothing can be conceived of from nothing, everything that can be must have a ground <note>original: "Grund" or "raison." This refers to the Principle of Sufficient Reason—the idea that there must be a logical explanation for why things are the way they are.</note> from which one can see why it is possible rather than impossible. This will be further proven in its proper place (§. 30. 31. *Metaphysics* <note>original abbreviation: "Met."</note>).

<margin>
Knowledge
of a
philosopher.

§. 5. In this manner, a philosopher <note>original: "Welt-Weiser"; literally "one wise in the ways of the world."</note> must not only know that something is possible, but also be able to show the ground why it is possible (§. 1. 2). For example, it is not enough for a philosopher to know that it can rain; rather, he must also be able to say how it happens that it rains, and from what causes it rains.

<margin>
How it is
distinguished
from
common
[knowledge].
</margin>
§. 6. By this, common knowledge is distinguished from the knowledge of a philosopher. Namely, one who does not understand philosophy <note>original: "Welt-Weißheit"</note> can indeed learn many things that are possible through experience; however, he does not know how to show the ground why they can be. For example, he learns from experience that it can rain, but cannot say how it happens that it rains, nor show the causes why it rains.

<summary>This page introduces the Principle of Sufficient Reason, arguing that everything must have a "ground" explaining its possibility. It distinguishes between the "common man," who knows things through experience, and the "philosopher," who understands the underlying causes and reasons for those things.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from §. 6 on the previous page regarding the distinction between common knowledge and philosophical knowledge. The text begins mid-word, completing the sentence about the benefits of a philosopher’s insight into specific circumstances.</meta>

### Concerning Philosophy.

<margin>
[of a]
Philosopher.
</margin>
[...of a philosopher] can proceed under certain circumstances, the person who only possesses a common knowledge <note>original: "gemeine Erkantniß"</note> of it may frequently overlook a detail, and then present as a universal truth something that only occurs in specific instances. Experience teaches this sufficiently. **For example**, one sees that one becomes compassionate toward a person in need when one recognizes their misery, and therefore imagines it to be a general rule that if one wishes to make someone compassionate, one need only present the plight of the wretched to them. In the same way, one sees in gardens that **rosemary** is propagated when young twigs are cut off and stuck with their lower part into the earth. One would, however, be greatly deceived if one tried to do this with all plants that are perennial.

In contrast, a **philosopher** <note>original: "Welt-Weiser"; literally "one wise in the ways of the world."</note> does not need to fear that he will apply his principles incorrectly, because he knows the **cause** why and when they must hold true (§. 6.). As in the first example: that the representation of the misery of the wretched only then creates compassion when the mind is previously inclined to take pleasure in the happiness of the other; and in the second example: that a twig stuck into the earth will strike roots <note>The text ends mid-sentence here; "Wurzeln" is completed by the catchword below.</note> when a node is placed in the earth, the twig does not easily wither, and the bark from the breaking through...

<meta>catchword: roots.</meta>

<summary>This page explains why philosophical knowledge is superior to common experience: a philosopher understands the specific causes and conditions (such as why rosemary roots or why people feel compassion) rather than making false generalizations.</summary>

Objection against the definition of philosophy.

§. 8. Perhaps some will wonder why **philosophy** <note>original: "Welt-Weißheit"; literally "world-wisdom." In this period, philosophy encompassed all systematic knowledge, including the natural sciences.</note> should extend to all possible things, since even the wisest person under the sun cannot boast of having understood more than a very small part of it. <margin>
Answer to the same.

§. 9. To those who have these thoughts, I offer the consideration that it is indeed much more advisable to tailor the description of philosophy according to its greatest **perfection** <note>original: "Vollkommenheit"</note>—that which it is capable of reaching—rather than according to one's own limited understanding or the concepts attained by another man. For in this way, no unnecessary **limits** <note>original: "Schrancken"</note> are set upon knowledge, which might otherwise discourage many from contemplating matters further, and consequently cause many useful **discoveries** <note>original: "Erfindungen"; in this context, it refers to both scientific discoveries and technical inventions.</note> to be left behind. Rather, everyone is encouraged to go further than their predecessors, seeing that so much still remains to be discovered. One will

# Preface <note>original: "Vorbericht"; an introductory report or foreword.</note>

Wisdom <note>The text continues from the previous page: "...whether we are fit for World-Wisdom."</note> are fit, or not; this shall be our first task: that we learn to recognize the powers of the human understanding and their proper use in the discovery of truth. The part of philosophy <note>original: "Welt-Weisheit"</note> in which this is shown is called **Logic**, or the **Art of Reason**, or also the **Doctrine of Reason** <note>original: "Vernunfft-Lehre."</note>.

<margin>Second Part.</margin>
§. 11. Among those things which are possible, one must necessarily be independent <note>original: "selbständig"; referring to a self-subsisting being that exists by its own nature rather than depending on another.</note>, for otherwise something would be possible for which no reason <note>original: "Grund"</note> could be shown as to why it exists, which would contradict what was already confirmed above (§. 4) <note>This refers to the Principle of Sufficient Reason, a cornerstone of 18th-century German philosophy which states that nothing exists without a reason why it is so and not otherwise.</note>. This independent being we call **God**: the other things, which have their reason <note>original: "raison"; French for "reason" or "ground."</note> for why they exist within this independent being, are called **creatures** <note>original: "Creaturen"; in this period, the term referred to all created things (objects, plants, animals, humans), not just living animals.</note>. Since philosophy now shows the reason why something can exist (§. 5), it is only right that the doctrine of **God**, or the independent being, be undertaken first, before one applies oneself to a precise knowledge of created things—though we do not deny that a person must first have a common knowledge <note>original: "gemeine Erkäntnis"; everyday, non-scientific awareness.</note> of them, which, however, he does not need to obtain from philosophy, as we arrive at it through daily experience from youth onward (§. 6). The part of philosophy in which [we learn] of **God** and

Creatures <note>In this context, "creatures" refers to anything created by God, including inanimate objects, plants, and minerals, not just animals.</note> manifest their activity either through motion, or through thoughts. The former we call **bodies**; the latter **spirits**. Since **World-Wisdom** <note>philosophy</note> endeavors to show the correct ground <note>original: "Grund"; the underlying reason or principle for why something is the way it is.</note> of all things, it must investigate the powers and effects of those things which perform their functions through motion, as well as those others which are conscious of themselves through their thoughts. Thus, it shows what is possible in the world through the powers of **bodies** as well as **spirits**. That part of **World-Wisdom** in which one explains what is possible through the powers of **spirits** is called **Pneumatology** <note>The study of spiritual beings, including the human soul and angels.</note> or the **Doctrine of Spirits**: the other part, however, in which one shows what is possible through the power of **bodies**, receives the name of **Physics**, or **Natural Science**, or also the **Doctrine of Nature**.

# Thoughts

## The Powers of the Human Understanding and Their Proper Use in the Knowledge of Truth.

## Chapter 1.
### On the Concepts of Things.

->§. 1.<-

<margin>What sensation is?</margin>
Everyone perceives within themselves that they sense many things. I say, however, that we sense something when we are conscious of it as being present to us. Thus we sense pain, sound, light, and our own thoughts.

->§. 2.<-

<margin>What is called a thought.</margin>
That activity of the soul <note>original: "Seele"; in the context of 18th-century philosophy, this refers to the mind or the seat of conscious activity.</note> through which we are conscious, we call a **thought**: for everyone says they are thinking nothing at the time when they believe they are conscious of nothing. <meta>This page continues the introductory definitions of Christian Wolff’s logical system. ### Chapter 1.

In this manner, sensations are thoughts of things present to us. We are explaining nothing further here than words, so that everyone may recognize in what sense we use them. The subjects themselves, which are signified by these words, I have detailed in my *Metaphysics* <note>original: "Metaphysick"; referring to Wolff’s *Vernünftige Gedanken von Gott, der Welt und der Seele des Menschen* (Rational Thoughts on God, the World, and the Soul of Man), which provides the ontological foundation for this logical treatise.</note>.

<margin>What is called the senses.</margin>
§. 3. The faculty <term>faculty|Vermögen; the inherent power or ability of the mind to perform a specific function</term> of immediately sensing things that are outside of us bears the name of the **senses**, of which one usually counts five: namely, seeing, hearing, feeling <note>In this context, "feeling" (Fühlen) refers to the sense of touch.</note>, smelling, and tasting.

<margin>What a concept is.</margin>
§. 4. I call a **concept** <term>concept|Begriff; the mental grasp or representation of an object</term> every representation of a thing in our thoughts. For example: I have a concept of the sun when I can represent it to myself in my thoughts, either through an image, as when I see it present before me, or through mere words, or even other signs, such as the symbol ☉ in astronomy <note>original: "Sternkunst"; literally "star-art," the contemporary term for the science of astronomy.</note>. By this, I indicate what I have perceived of the sun: that it is the body shining brightly in the sky by day, which dazzles the eyes and makes it warm and light upon the earth. Likewise, I have a concept of a wedding when I can represent to myself in my thoughts, as if in an image, how two persons fulfill their promise to marry one another according to the custom usual in a country.

<summary>Wolff defines sensations as thoughts of present objects and introduces the five senses as the faculties for perceiving external things. He also defines a "concept" as any mental representation, whether formed through direct images, words, or symbolic signs.</summary>
<keywords>sensation, senses, concept, mental representation, faculty, astronomy, wedding, Christian Wolff</keywords>

§. 5. Since the senses now give occasion for thoughts of things that are outside of us (§. 2. 3), they lead us to a concept of them. # On the Concepts of Things.

triangle and a square. The triangle presents itself to me quite differently than the square. When I now ask myself what moves me to consider the triangle as something different from the square, I find that it happens because the triangle is enclosed in three lines, but the square in four lines. What I find in the triangle, I do not encounter in the square; and what is perceived in the square is not that which the triangle shows. One cannot take one for the other. And thereby one notices the difference between both figures. So if I ask what moves me to say that it is light; I find it is this: that I can see the things around me. Accordingly, light is that which makes the surrounding things visible. In the same way, we find that <term>Buying</term> means acquiring a thing belonging to another for a piece of money agreed upon by him; <term>Full-blooded</term> <note>original: "Vollblütig"; a historical medical term for a "plethoric" constitution, then believed to be an unhealthy excess of blood</note> is he who has more blood than is necessary for the maintenance of health; and a <term>Swelling</term> <note>original: "Geschwulst"; used here in the general historical sense of any abnormal enlargement of body tissue</note> is an extraordinary thickness in the fleshy parts of the body, and so forth.

What a clear and obscure concept is.
</margin>

§. 9. If the <term>concept</term> <note>original: "Begriff"; in this context, the mental representation or definition of a thing</note> we have is sufficient to recognize things when they occur—such as when we know it is the very same thing that bears this or another name, which we have seen in this or that place—then it is clear. Conversely, it is obscure <note>original: "dunckel"; literally "dark," referring to a thought that is too vague to allow for identification</note> if it does not suffice to recognize the thing again. Thus we have a clear concept of colors, for we can recognize them and distinguish them from one another when they appear. Likewise, we have a clear concept of anger, for we can recognize when someone is angry. ### On the Concepts of Things.

<margin>Degrees of obscurity.</margin>
§. 10. However, this obscurity has different degrees. For sometimes, regarding a present object, we can recall many <term>characteristics</term> <note>original: "Merckmahle"; the distinguishing features or attributes that allow us to identify and define a thing</note> that were also to be found in another; at other times, we recall very few. For example, regarding a foreign plant, we might recall that the other one, of which we have an obscure concept, had the same long, pointed, and jagged leaves as the one currently before us; but it does not occur to us whether it also looked the same in all other respects as the leaves of the plant standing before our eyes. Depending on whether we can recall many or few such characteristics, our concept is accordingly either slightly or very obscure.

<margin>Examples of obscure concepts.</margin>
§. 11. Thus, we have very obscure concepts of all those words whose meaning we do not rightly know, even though we are familiar with their sound, and when we pay attention to them, they do not leave us entirely without thoughts. For example: everyone is familiar with the word **Virtue**: and since one calls many human actions **virtuous**, and many others **unvirtuous**, then

<margin>Caution is necessary regarding obscure concepts.</margin>
§. 12. We must, however, take great care not to judge as if others also did not associate a clear concept with words for which we have only an obscure one. For otherwise, we would consider obscure in and of itself what is merely a lack of our own knowledge, and reject as empty sound words that have their correct meaning. Thus, some reject the *centripetal forces* <note>original: "vires centripetas"</note> and *centrifugal forces* <note>original: "vires centrifugas"</note> of today’s <term>philosophers</term> <note>original: "Welt-Weisen"; literally "world-wise ones," the common Enlightenment term for philosophers and scientists</note>, because they do not actually know what such forces signify. Others mock the infinitely small in geometry because they do not grasp what this term is actually meant to say. ### The 1st Chapter.

encounter **distinct** concepts. On the other hand, our concept of the **red color** is indeed clear, but nevertheless **indistinct**. For we can indeed recognize the red color when it appears before us; yet we are unable to say by what means we recognize it. Thus, many people have a clear but indistinct concept of the various types of **trees** and **plants**, for they can recognize one species from another and distinguish them all from each other quite well; yet they cannot actually say wherein the difference consists. An indistinct concept of this kind is also the **whistling of the wind**, the **murmuring of water**, and the **roaring of waves**. Indeed, we notice the difference in **taste** and **smell** only clearly, but not distinctly.

<margin>
How each of the two is to be imparted to another.
</margin>

§. 14. Therefore, one can impart a **distinct concept** to another with mere words; an **indistinct** one, however, can only be conveyed by presenting the thing itself to the person. For example: if someone is blind, one may preach to him whatever one wishes, yet he will never obtain the concept of red or any other color that we possess.

The difference between comprehensive and non-comprehensive concepts.

§. 15. A **distinct concept** is either **comprehensive** or **non-comprehensive**. For example, one has **comprehensive concepts** of **living knowledge**, **usufruct** or **use**, **colic**, the **understanding**, **dew**, **avarice**, etc., when one can say: **living knowledge** is a type of knowledge that influences the will. **Use** <note>original: "Nutzung"; here referring to the legal concept of usufruct</note> is the right to obtain an advantage from someone else's property according to one's pleasure, provided that the thing itself remains in its original state from one time to the next. **Colic** is a sensitive and persistent pain in the intestines. The **understanding** is a faculty for thinking about the possible. **Dew** is a collection of subtle vapors that, in the absence of the sun, gradually fall from the air and attach themselves to the upper surface of objects on the ground. **Avarice** is a desire to have more than one needs for one's basic necessities and can acquire according to one's circumstances, etc.

### Chapter 1.

On the other hand, the **Cartesians** <note>followers of the philosopher René Descartes</note> have a **non-comprehensive** concept of the **body** <note>original: "Cörper"</note> when they say it is a being that is extended in length, width, and thickness. For by this **characteristic** <note>original: "Merckmahl"</note> alone, one cannot yet distinguish the body from **space** <note>original: "Raum"</note>, which is why the Cartesians consider them to be the same. Many more examples of non-comprehensive concepts can be found in the writings of most scholars.

<margin>
What a complete and incomplete concept is.
</margin>

§. 16. Finally, a distinct **concept** is either **complete** or **incomplete**. Our concept is **complete** <note>original: "vollständig"</note> when we also have clear and distinct concepts of the characteristics by which the thing is recognized. Conversely, it is **incomplete** <note>original: "unvollständig"</note> if we have only indistinct concepts of the characteristics by which the thing is recognized. For example: if a person can not only say that a **striking clock** <note>original: "Schlag-Uhr"</note> is a **machine** that indicates the hours by striking a bell, but also has distinct concepts of the striking of the bell, the hour, and the indication, then his concept of the striking clock is complete. <header>
# Of the Concepts of Things.

...the concept of an hour can again be broken down into the concept of the number 24, the concept of a part, and finally the concept of a day. And since these concepts in turn contain their own **characteristics** <note>original: "Merckmahle"; in 18th-century logic, these are the individual components or "marks" that make up a definition</note>, they can be analyzed further once again. The same applies to the other examples given previously.

<margin>How far concepts are to be analyzed.</margin>
§. 18. However, it is by no means necessary, and even quite rarely possible, for us to bring this **analysis** <term>analysis: the process of breaking a complex idea into its simplest parts; the German "Zergliederung" literally means "dismemberment" or "segmentation"</term> to a final end—that is, to carry it out until we reach those concepts which can no longer be analyzed in and of themselves because they no longer contain several distinct elements within them. Instead, we can be satisfied when we have brought the analysis far enough to achieve our purpose. Specifically, we use a concept either to indicate to others what we mean, or to derive a **proof** from it. Thus, in the first case, we have reached our goal when the other person fully understands us; that is, when we have carried the analysis out to those characteristics of which the other person has entirely clear concepts. In the second case, however, we have succeeded when, within the proof, nothing remains left to be proven—a point which will be better understood later. For example: **Euclid** <note>Euclid of Alexandria (c. 300 BCE), the Greek mathematician whose "Elements" served as the primary textbook for logic and geometry</note> accepts the words **equality**, **greater**, and **smaller** without explanation and...

How a distinct concept is attained.

-># Of the Concepts of Things.<-

For example: Titius <note>a standard placeholder name in Latin and German academic texts, much like "John Doe" today</note>, who until now has applied himself to his studies with laudable diligence so that he might expect a good **advancement** <term>advancement: a professional promotion or career appointment (Beförderung)</term> in the future, hears of a **position** <note>original: "condition"; in this era, this specifically referred to a job post, often as a tutor or secretary in a noble house</note> with a prominent man who can help him toward an advancement if he enjoys his favor. As he thus views the position as a means to his advancement, he wills to accept it. Here we find: 1. a thing which Titius considers within himself, namely the position being offered to him; 2. the **mental representation** <term>mental representation: the way an object or idea is pictured in the mind (Vorstellung)</term> he forms of it regarding himself, namely that it is a means to his advancement—that is, it is **good** for him; 3. the state of the **mind** <term>mind: the inner disposition or "heart" (Gemüth)</term> during this representation, namely that he not only takes pleasure in the position for the sake of this representation, but also senses an **inclination** toward it within himself. Now, if one takes all of this together, one sees that the **will** is an inclination of the mind toward a thing that we [represent to ourselves as...]

<margin>How a complete concept is obtained.</margin>

Thus it is indeed possible that we can have a **distinct**, even **complete concept** <term>complete concept: an exhaustive understanding where one knows not just the parts of a thing, but also the components of those parts, down to their most basic elements</term> of a **table**; yet many have only an **indistinct** one, even though it stands before their eyes every day. It is also possible to obtain a distinct concept of **time**; for one can certainly recognize that which allows it to be distinguished from other things: yet most have only an indistinct concept of it, because they do not pay attention to what they actually find in their thoughts and the visible world, through which they reach the **knowledge** <note>original: "Erkäntnis"; in this context, it refers to the process of cognition or certain understanding</note> of time. ## Chapter 1.

...can no longer distinguish. In the same way, our concepts of colors, types of taste, types of smell, and likewise of sound, remain **indistinct**, because within them are to be found countless little details which make it impossible for us to distinguish them from one another.

<margin>The usefulness of magnifying glasses and telescopes.</margin>
§ 22. Therefore, **magnifying glasses** <term>magnifying glasses: "Vergrösserungs-Gläser," a term used here to refer to early microscopes</term> often help us toward a **distinct concept** that we would otherwise never obtain. For example, one finds that the **pith** <note>original: "Marck"; the soft, spongy tissue in the center of plant stems</note> in plants is a cluster of tiny vesicles; that the sparks which one strikes from steel with a flint are actually glowing little pieces of steel and stone, which sometimes melt and transform into glass. One also learns that the burning of nettles is in fact nothing other than a stinging, which happens by means of subtle spines that stand here and there upon the leaves. More examples can be found in **Leeuwenhoek’s** letters <note>Antonie van Leeuwenhoek (1632–1723), a Dutch scientist who improved the microscope and was the first to observe "animalcules" or microorganisms</note> and in **Hooke’s** *Small-Drawings* <note>original Latin title: "Micrographia"; the 1665 masterpiece by Robert Hooke which featured detailed illustrations of objects seen through a microscope</note>. Similar help is also sometimes provided to us by **telescopes** <note>original: "Fern-Gläser"</note>, to which we owe much in our knowledge of the **structure of the universe** <note>original: "Welt-Gebäude," literally "world-building"</note>. For example, we now know that the **Milky Way** is a cluster of small stars; that the moon’s surface is mountainous and not flat; and that **Venus** and **Mercury** have the phases of a waxing and waning moon.

<margin>Why concepts deteriorate.</margin>
§. 24. Because we easily tend to forget those things that we either do not think of often, or what we do not reflect upon enough, or even those things from whose contemplation we immediately turn to something else; thus the **marks** <term>marks: "Merckmahle," the specific features or characteristics used to define and distinguish a concept</term> by which we distinguish things from one another can also slip from our minds. Consequently, **complete concepts** can become **incomplete**, **distinct** ones **indistinct**, and **indistinct** ones **obscure** <note>original: "dunckel"; the author is describing a hierarchy of mental clarity where "obscure" is the least useful level of understanding</note>: indeed, a concept can be lost entirely, so that we are no longer able to represent the thing to ourselves in our **thoughts** in any way during its absence.

<margin>How to prevent this.</margin>
§. 25. So that concepts, once attained, do not deteriorate, we must represent them to ourselves frequently and take diligent care that we do not confuse ourselves with many things at once. Especially in the sciences, it is advisable that we write down the distinct concepts we have once attained: for on the pap- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "papiere" (paper).</note>

# On the Concepts of Things.

One sees, however, without my reminding, that one can omit as much as one pleases of those things by which the matter is determined in itself, and thus always ascend to more general concepts. 

For example: In the concept of a **rectilinear** <term>rectilinear: "geradelinichten," consisting of or bounded by straight lines</term> triangle—that it is a space enclosed in three straight lines—I can omit the type of lines; thus I am left with the concept of a triangle in general. Or: I omit the number of lines; then there remains for me the concept of a rectilinear figure.

# Chapter 1

...[discern] what has been said regarding the types of laws (Section 17, *Moral Philosophy* <note>original: "Mor." refers to Wolff’s *Philosophia Moralis* or Ethics</note>) and of conscience (Section 74 and following, *Moral Philosophy*), as well as the various forms of government (Section 233 and following, *Politics* <note>original: "Polit." refers to Wolff’s *Politica*</note>), if one considers it in a proper manner—that is, if one adopts certain forms of government that existed either previously or still today and derives from them the universal concepts that I have provided.

<margin>Why some concepts are universal.</margin>
§. 28. In view of the fact that a concept applies to all things of a certain kind, it is called **universal** <term>universal: "allgemein," referring to a general idea that covers an entire group rather than a single specific object</term>; and therefore, the more universal it is, the less specific detail it contains within itself. Consequently, universal concepts are easier to contemplate the more types of things they apply to.

<margin>The utility of universal concepts.</margin>
§. 29. One strives for universal concepts because they expand the boundaries of our knowledge beyond measure. For whatever is derived from a universal concept applies to all things contained under it—for example <term>z. E.: abbreviation for "zum Exempel," meaning for example</term>, whatever is derived from the concept of a rectilinear triangle applies to all rectilinear triangles; whatever flows from the concept of an **affect** <term>affect: "Affect," an 18th-century term for an emotion, passion, or mental state</term> applies to all affects; whatever is concluded from the concept of a fluid body applies to all fluid bodies, and so forth.

<meta>catchword: §. 30.</meta>

<margin>
The third way to arrive at a concept.
</margin>

§. 30. Just as we arrive at new concepts by omitting one thing or another, which determines the thing in itself; so, on the other hand, we can also determine other things that are not yet determined, or even determine that which is already determined in a different way <term>determine: "determiniren," meaning to define or specify a concept by adding particular characteristics</term>. For example: In the concept of a rectilinear triangle—that it is a space enclosed by three straight lines—the size of the lines is not determined. If I therefore stipulate that all three lines should be equal to one another, I obtain the concept of an **equilateral** triangle. If I put curved lines in the place of straight lines, I have a **curvilinear** triangle. In the same way, if I further determine—within the previously mentioned concept of **joy** (Section 24)—from whom I have received the good that I enjoy, then from this arises the concept of **gratitude**, insofar as it is an **affect** <term>affect: "Affect," an 18th-century term for a strong emotion or mental state</term> that is excited within us when we imagine that another person is the cause of our happiness. In the same manner, one can derive concepts of countless types of **understanding**, as well as of different types of **virtues** and **vices**: namely, if in the first case [one considers] the way and manner in which possible things are represented (for the understanding

<margin>What an explanation and a description are.</margin>
§ 16. If a distinct concept is exhaustive—that is (§ 15), if it is of such a nature that it applies to no more things than those of one kind, and they can therefore be distinguished by it from all others of their like at all times—then I call it an **explanation** <note>original: "Erklärung." In this philosophical context, it specifically refers to a "definition" that captures the essence of a thing.</note>, because it makes the matter clear to me so that I can recognize it. However, if it is only sufficient for a time to recognize something from other present things under certain circumstances, then I call it **a description**.

In such a way, the concept of a **lunar eclipse**—that it is a deprivation of light during a full moon—is an explanation, for by it I can correctly distinguish the lunar eclipse from all other celestial events. In the same way, an explanation of the **understanding** <term>understanding: "Verstand," the cognitive faculty of the mind used for forming concepts and reasoning.</term> is that it is a power to clearly represent what is possible, for by this I can distinguish it from other things found in the **soul**. And many other examples belong here,

§ 37. § 38. Both, however, must have such characteristics as are known to the person to whom I wish to explain or describe a thing. For otherwise, it would not be possible to recognize the thing from the given explanation or description.

# On the Concepts of Things.

<margin>
The nature
of explanations
in particular.
</margin>
§ 39. Therefore, in the **sciences** <term>sciences: "Wissenschafften," referring to any systematic branch of knowledge, including what we now call the humanities and social sciences.</term>, nothing must be included in an **explanation** that is not either rightfully presumed to be known to someone, or has been previously explained. For example: if I say in **Politics** (§ 234) that **monarchy** is a form of government where the care of the common welfare and security is the **absolute** <note>original: "schlechter dinges," an archaic form of "schlechterdings," meaning simply, absolutely, or purely.</note> duty of a single person; then it has been shown previously what a form of government is (Politics, § 234), what the common welfare and security consists of (Politics, § 213), and what a person is (**Metaphysics**, § 924); and it will be further explained (Politics, § 234) what the words "absolute duty" are intended to mean.

I can, however, rightfully presume that something is known to someone if either no one is fit to acquire a science unless they are first well-versed in another in which that thing is explained; or because we have it daily before our eyes. For example: whoever wishes to learn **astronomy** must first understand **geometry**. Therefore, it is not wrong if I use such words in astronomical explanations, without further elucidation, that are commonly explained in geometry. Whoever wishes to learn **ethics** <term>ethics: "Sitten-Lehre," the study of moral principles and human conduct.</term> as a science, wherein one [derives] the nature of virtues and vices from the internal knowledge of the human soul...

# On the Concepts of Things.

...if I have not previously explained what these chains should signify.

<margin>Difference between explanations.</margin>
§ 41. Explanations, however, define either **words** or **things**; therefore, they are quite appropriately divided into **word-explanations** <term>Word-explanation: Historically called a "nominal definition," this identifies a thing by describing its outward characteristics or its name.</term> and **thing-explanations** <term>Thing-explanation: Historically called a "real definition," this explains the internal mechanics or the "how" of a thing's existence.</term>. The former consist of a recounting of several properties by which a thing is distinguished from all others of its kind; the latter show the way and manner in which something is possible. For example: If I say a **clockwork** is a machine that indicates the hours, I am explaining the word "clockwork." However, if I show from what wheels and other accessories it is assembled, I am explaining the thing. In the same way, if I say **reason** is an insight into the connection of truths, I am explaining the word "reason." But if I show how such an insight is possible through the powers of the soul, I am explaining the thing.

<margin>How to construct word-explanations.</margin>
§ 42. Thus, nothing can be included in a word-explanation except that which always belongs to a thing. For example: In **gratitude**, there is always a remembrance of benefits received. Therefore, this is included in its explanation (§ 469 Met. <note>original: "Met.", referring to the author's previous work on Metaphysics.</note>). In order to discover such things, one must carefully investigate why this or that belongs to a thing.

He uses clocks, reason, and gratitude as primary examples to illustrate how definitions must be grounded in permanent characteristics.</summary>
<keywords>nominal definition, real definition, clockwork, reason, gratitude, metaphysics, logic, properties</keywords>

# Chapter 1.

<margin>thus</margin>
<margin>concerning the spirit</margin> <note>original: "v. d. Geist". The author likely refers to the human mind or intellect.</note>
<margin>thus</margin>
For if I find the **ground** <term>Ground: In 18th-century logic, this refers to the "sufficient reason" or the underlying cause that makes a statement or thing true.</term> of it in the matter itself, then I am assured that it belongs to it constantly; on the other hand, if the ground is to be sought outside of the thing, then it can only belong to it under certain circumstances. Since no thing can act directly upon another unless it is close enough to it, I must bring it out of the proximity of the things among which it is currently found and into the proximity of others. It will then soon be revealed whether something belongs to it only for the sake of certain circumstances, or not. For example: I suppose that someone has not yet seen wax and finds a piece lying on a window on a hot summer day. At that time, it will be quite soft. If I now wish to know whether the cause of why the wax is soft is to be sought within it or outside of it, I need only bring it into cold air, such as into a cellar.
<margin>thus</margin>
Here it will become hard; and thus I will see that wax is not always soft, but only in the warmth.
<margin>conversely</margin> <note>original: "resp.", an abbreviation for "respective" or "respondent," used here to introduce a contrasting example.</note>
In contrast, a stone may be in warm or cold air, yet it remains hard. And therefore, the cause of its hardness is to be sought not outside of it, but within it.

### Concerning the Concepts of Things.

<margin>Definitions.</margin>
...to signify that which is to be explained. <note>The text continues from the previous page, completing the thought that a definition should not simply use synonyms of the word it is trying to define.</note> For then I would become no wiser through the definition than I was before, nor could I prove anything from it, much less be able to discover something unknown. For example: If someone defines the **infinite** <term>Infinite: In 18th-century logic, defining the "infinite" was a classic problem; philosophers often argued whether it was a positive concept or merely a negative one (the "lack of limits").</term> as that which has **no limits**, he has not yet provided a distinct concept of the infinite: for it is no clearer to have "no limits" than it is to be "infinite." Again, if someone says the attractive force of a magnet is a force by which the magnet pulls iron toward itself; then he says with many words exactly what he had already said with one.

<margin>The second error.</margin>
§ 44. One must also take care that one thing is not defined by the other <note>This refers to circular reasoning, or "circulus in demonstrando," where two terms are used to define each other without ever reaching a foundational concept.</note>; otherwise, the concept does not become truly distinct. For example: If I define an **hour** as the twenty-fourth part of a **day**, but then define the **day** as a period of twenty-four **hours**; then I understand neither correctly what a day is, nor what an hour is. Likewise, if I said a **scholar** is a person who has **studied**, and if someone asked me what it means to study, I answered: "to strive to become scholarly"; then I would know neither what studying is, nor what a scholar is called. In this matter, one can very easily make a mistake when dealing with things that are somewhat far removed from our senses—

In such cases, it can easily happen that one explains virtue as a skill of directing one's actions according to the law of nature, and subsequently explains the law of nature as a guideline for virtuous actions: both of which explanations cannot hold true at the same time. <note>This is a classic example of a "vicious circle" in logic: you cannot use A to define B if you also use B to define A.</note>

<margin>
In what the essence of a thing consists.

§ 48. The first thing that can be thought of concerning a thing, and in which the reason for the rest that pertains to it is to be found, is called the **essence**. Now, nothing can be thought of concerning a thing sooner than how it originated or became what it is. Therefore, one understands the **essence** of a thing when one clearly grasps how it became what it is, or in what manner and way it is possible. From this it further follows that the explanations of things place their essence before our eyes. For example: I understand the essence of a clock if I clearly grasp from what wheels and other attachments it...

# Chapter 1.

In the same way, we understand the essence of the eye when we are acquainted with what parts it is composed of and how the eye is assembled from them. And the same applies to the knowledge of the essence of our body in general.

<margin>
What is required for the explanation of a thing.
</margin>
§ 49. Since the explanations of a thing should show how it originates (§ 41), two things are required for this. First, one must know what things are necessary for it if it is to come into being. Second, it must be known what each of them contributes to that end. For example: if someone were to explain how **vapors** <note>original: "Dünste." In 18th-century science, this referred to the process of evaporation and the formation of mist or steam.</note> originate, he must not only know that water, heat, and air are required for them; but also understand what each of these three contributes to the vapors.

<meta>This page continues the logical method for discovering the nature of things. While the previous page applied this to mathematical curves and metaphysical concepts (like Leibniz's monads), this page provides a practical example from physics regarding the formation of vapors.</meta>

# Chapter 1.

<margin>
How to find the [definitions] of things.
</margin>
...to find. One must seek clear concepts of all characteristics <term>characteristics: "Merckmahle," the distinguishing features or attributes that constitute a concept</term> that are contained therein. By considering these, it will soon become apparent what is required to form the thing. If we then further review our knowledge <term>knowledge: "Erkántniß," referring here to the body of cognitive understanding or prior learning</term> which we have acquired at another time, and similar things occur to us as those required; then we have found the explanation of the thing. But if we can recall nothing of the sort, we see that its discovery does not lie within our power. 

For example: A nominal definition <term>nominal definition: "Wort-Erklärung," literally a "word-explanation," which describes what a word means rather than the internal possibility of the thing itself</term> of vapors is given to me, stating that they are small particles of water which rise up into the air; I am tasked with finding how these are formed. To that end, I recall everything that I have clearly recognized concerning water, air, and likewise the rising of bodies <note>original: "Cörper," meaning physical objects or matter</note> in fluid substances; such as, that there is much air in water, that water is expanded into little bubbles <note>original: "Bläßgen," a diminutive of bubble</note> by the air, that heat expands air, and that lighter bodies rise up in a heavier fluid substance. If I now compare these things with one another, I easily see that when the sun shines upon the water, or the water is otherwise placed in a warm location, the air within it is expanded, and subsequently small bubbles are formed, which are not only lighter than water, but also lighter than the air itself—or at least light enough to be carried by it.

<summary>This page explains a systematic method for scientific discovery: by breaking a definition into its required characteristics and comparing them with previously known physical principles, one can deduce how natural phenomena, like water vapor, are formed.</summary>
<keywords>nominal definition, scientific method, characteristics, vapors, physics of water, knowledge acquisition, air expansion</keywords>

<margin>How one can make this easier.</margin>
§. 55. It cannot be denied that it is somewhat difficult to find the explanations of things in the manner prescribed; for it requires not only that one already knows much, but one must also have practiced quite a bit in reflection <term>reflection: "Nachsinnen," the deliberate act of deep, logical thought or meditation on a subject</term>. Therefore, beginners should not trouble themselves with this. However, when they encounter explanations of things, they can practice with great benefit by investigating how these could have been discovered according to the prescribed method. And since no other way is necessary here than the one by which one arrives at the knowledge <term>knowledge: "Erkänntnis," often meaning "cognition" or the cognitive grasp of a truth</term> of one truth through another, and finds the cause of natural effects <term>effects: "Würckungen," the outcomes or operations produced by natural forces</term> in the solution of problems, one will gain even more light <note>The author uses "light" (Licht) in the Enlightenment sense of clarity and intellectual understanding.</note> on this matter if one carefully reads what has been provided regarding these pieces in Chapter 6 below.
