## Chapter 2.

# On the Use of Words.

->§. 1.<-

<margin>What words are.</margin>
Through words, we are accustomed to making our thoughts known to others. And so they are nothing other than signs <note>original: "Zeichen." In 18th-century philosophy, a "sign" is a bridge between the internal mind and the external world.</note> of our thoughts, from which another person can recognize our thoughts. For example: If someone asks me what I am thinking about, and I answer "the sun," I use this word to make them understand what kind of thing I am currently representing <term>representing: "vorstelle," the act of placing an image or idea before the mind's eye</term> in my thoughts.

->§. 2.<-

<margin>When two people understand one another.</margin>
Thus, if two people speak with one another and one is to understand the other, it is required: 1. That the one who speaks is able to think of something with every word; 2. that the one who hears him speak can think of the very same thing with every word as what the other is thinking.

->§. 3.<-

<margin>Every word must have a meaning.</margin>
Accordingly, a certain concept <term>concept: "Begriff," a mental grasp or category of an object</term> must be linked with every word, and consequently, there must always be something which is signified by the word.

<note>original vocabulary: Wörter (words), Gedancken (thoughts), Zeichen (signs), Begriff (concept), Bedeutung (meaning)</note>

<meta>Catchword: §. 4. ben. (This catchword suggests the next section will begin with the word "ben," likely part of a word like "benennen" or "benöthigen").</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the fundamental nature of language, defining words as external signs for internal thoughts and outlining the requirements for clear communication.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy of language, communication, signs, mental representation, concepts, mutual understanding</keywords>

How one discovers whether they know what they are saying.

# On the Use of Words.

...hear this or another word (§. 4). However, I have no concept of the thing that is supposed to be indicated by this word.

<margin>Empty words can be understood.</margin>
§. 10. From this it is now clear as day that people can speak with one another and understand each other, and yet no one may have a concept of what they are saying or hearing, because they are speaking of nothing at all. Such discourses are not rare among the learned; they are especially frequent in the **natural philosophy** <note>original: "Natur-Lehre"</note> of the **schoolmen** <note>The "Schulweisen" or Scholastics, medieval and early modern philosophers whose highly technical language was often criticized by Enlightenment thinkers for being empty of real meaning.</note> (§. 7).

<margin>Words can signify something of which we have no concept.</margin>
§. 11. Just as there are words that have no concept, there can also be words through which something real is indicated, of which we have no clear and distinct concept, even if they are not unknown to us. For example: the word **lynx** <note>original: "Luchs"</note> signifies an animal that is well known to hunters and is famous for its sharp eyesight. Many know the word, but they have no clear, let alone distinct, concept of it.

<margin>The baselessness of those who reject the mysteries.</margin>
§. 12. Therefore, it does not follow that words with which we associate no clear concept mean nothing—as the enemies of the **Gospel** <note>original: "Evangelii"</note> claim when they dismiss the word **Trinity** <note>original: "Dreyeinigkeit"</note> and other words signifying mysteries as mere empty sounds.

<meta>catchword: §. 13,</meta>

<summary>This page argues that people often use words without having a true mental concept of their meaning, particularly in academic philosophy. It also defends religious concepts like the Trinity, noting that a lack of a clear human concept does not mean a word refers to nothing.</summary>

# Chapter 2.

<margin>Others may possess a concept for words that we do not.</margin>
§. 13. Much less does it follow that if we cannot associate a clear and distinct concept with a word, another person is incapable of doing so. In this regard, those who err are those who, in the **sciences** <note>original: "Wissenschafften." In the 18th century, this term referred to all systematic branches of knowledge, including philosophy and logic, rather than just the natural sciences.</note>, reject all words for which they cannot form a clear and distinct concept.

<margin>How they are correctly explained.</margin>
§. 14. Now, if another is to understand me, I must not use any word unless I am certain that he can not only have the concept I associate with it, but also that the word—as soon as he hears it and reflects upon it—evokes that same concept within him. For it happens very often that another person associates a completely different concept with a word than I do, even though he might just as well associate mine with it. For example: **Simplicius** <note>A traditional name for a "simple" or naive character in philosophical dialogues, often used as a foil for more sophisticated thinkers.</note>, who from his childhood has always read amusing books and attended many farces, imagines the ability to judge with **acumen** <note>original: "scharffsinnig zu urtheilen"</note> as the skill of pulling everything through the mud and making it look ridiculous. **Lynceus** <note>Named after the sharp-sighted Argonaut of Greek myth; here used to represent a person of deep intellectual insight.</note>, however, who has uncommonly exercised his understanding through thorough sciences, takes the ability to judge with acumen to be the skill of carrying out his conclusions in an orderly sequence during the proof of what he asserts, until he arrives at undeniable **ground-** <note>original: "Grün-" (catchword for "Gründe," meaning grounds, reasons, or foundations).</note>

<summary>The author argues that the validity of a technical term does not depend on an individual's personal understanding and emphasizes that effective communication requires words to trigger identical concepts in both the speaker and the listener.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the definition of judgment introduced on the previous page. It transitions from the simple act of perceiving a thing to the complex mental process of forming a proposition by connecting or separating different concepts.</meta>

<header>The 3rd Chapter.</header>
<meta>Catchword: strik-</meta>

...ding, when we say: this building is beautiful; that building is not built according to the rules of art <note>In the 18th century, "the rules of art" (Regeln der Kunst) referred to established classical principles of aesthetics and engineering.</note>. We judge iron when we say: iron can become red-hot; iron cannot swim. We judge a stone falling from a great height when we say: it could strike someone dead; it will not shatter. In short: we judge when we think to ourselves that something belongs to a thing, or does not.

<margin>What judging consists of.</margin>
§. 2. Therefore, when we judge, we link at least two concepts <term>original: "Begriffe"; the mental building blocks or ideas we have of objects or qualities.</term> together, or separate them from one another; namely, the concept of the thing about which we are judging, and the concept of that which is supposed to belong to it, or not. I say at least two: for others can also be associated with these two. For example, when I judge: this house is beautiful; I am connecting the concept of beauty with the concept of the house. Accordingly, there are no more than two concepts in this judgment. On the other hand, when I judge: a stone falling from a great height strikes one dead; then the concepts of height and falling are associated with the concept of the stone, and with the concept of strik- <note>The text cuts off here. The catchword "Werf" at the bottom of the original page indicates the next word is "striking" (werffen).</note>

<summary>This page explains that judgment is the mental act of connecting or separating concepts, such as attributing beauty to a house or weight to a stone. It emphasizes that while a simple judgment involves two concepts, more complex thoughts can involve many associated ideas.</summary>

<header>Of Propositions.</header>

...of striking <note>Continuing from the previous page: "...the concept of striking dead."</note>, the concept of death. In this manner, a judgment is either a connection or a separation of two or more concepts.

<margin>
What a proposition is, as well as an affirmative and a negative one.
</margin>
§ 3. Since we are now compelled to use words instead of concepts—partly when we reveal our judgment to others (§ 1, chap. 2), and partly also often when we wish to represent it to ourselves in thought (§ 5, chap. 2)—a judgment must be expressed by at least two words: one of which indicates the thing of which we are speaking, while the other signifies that which pertains to it. The former is called the subject <term>original: "Vorderglied"; literally the "front-member," referring to the subject of a logical statement.</term>; the latter, however, is called the predicate <term>original: "Hinterglied"; literally the "back-member," referring to the property or action being attributed to the subject.</term>. The speech through which we give it to be understood that something pertains to a thing or does not is called a proposition <term>original: "Satz"; a formal statement or logical sentence.</term>. Specifically, it is called an affirmative proposition when we say that something pertains to a thing; conversely, it is called a negative proposition when we say that something does not pertain to it. For example: If I say, "The sky is bright," I affirm something; but if I say, "The sky is not bright," I deny something.

<margin>
How one understands a proposition.
</margin>
§ 4. If someone wishes to understand a proposition, he must endeavor to obtain a concept for all the words contained within it. For words are placed in the stead of concepts (§ 2); I

<meta>catchword: understand-</meta>

<summary>This page defines a "proposition" as the verbal expression of a mental judgment, consisting of a subject and a predicate. It distinguishes between affirmative and negative propositions and explains that understanding a proposition requires understanding the concepts behind its words.</summary>
<keywords>proposition, judgment, subject, predicate, affirmative, negative, concepts, language and thought</keywords>

In the first case, then, that which pertains to one must

only some become warm, namely those which

the other two, however, they are called **particular**

the same way, this is a **particular proposition**: 
**Some stones** become warm, namely those which 
lie long in warmth. But it becomes a **general** 
one if I say: **All stones** that

The word **all** is thus the sign 
by which **general propositions** are indicated: 
which is also often omitted, 
as when I say: the **stone** is 
heavy: the **warm stone** makes warm: 
the **stone**, which lies long in the warmth, 
becomes warm.

<margin>Answering an objection.</margin>
§. 7. It appears as if such an <term>analysis</term> <note>original: "Zergliederung"; literally a "dismembering" or "deconstruction" of a logical statement into its component parts.</note> does not apply in every case. For if the reason why something pertains to a thing (or not) must be sought in what is **essential** <note>original: "eigenthümlich"; referring to the intrinsic or "peculiar" properties that belong to the very nature of an object.</note> to it, then whatever pertains to it does so directly and thus without any **condition**. The same applies to that which cannot pertain to it. **E.G.** Every **triangle** has three angles, without any condition. **God is almighty**, without any condition. It is not difficult to answer this. Precisely that which is **essential** to the thing provides the **condition**: for if that same quality were not essential to it, then the **assertion** would not hold true. ### Chapter 3.

...**almighty**, (since by **GOD** I understand a being who possesses all **perfections** in the highest degree) I think of two things regarding **GOD**: first, that He possesses all perfections in the highest degree; and second, that He is **almighty**, that is, that He can do all that He wills. I think of the latter for the sake of the former. 

Conversely, with the <term>proposition</term> <note>original: "Satz"; a formal logical statement or claim.</note> "no cold stone can make something warm," I also carry two thoughts: namely, I think of being cold, and of making something warm. However, the second thought does not take place if I admit the first. One of the two cancels out the other. 

Now, if two thoughts are so constituted that the second necessarily occurs when one harbors the first, or that I must necessarily think of the second if I think of the first regarding a thing, then the thoughts <term>agree</term> <note>original: "übereinstimmen"; to be in harmony or logically consistent.</note> with one another. If, however, I cannot possibly think of the second regarding a thing if I think of the first, then my thoughts **clash** <note>original: "streiten"; literally to "fight" or "quarrel," here describing logical conflict.</note> with each other, or one **contradicts** the other.

### The 3rd Chapter.

of the other kind is when I say: He who wants to make someone joyful must inquire as to what they would like to have, increase their appetite for it even further, and then unexpectedly bestow the desired thing. The first kind we shall call **Theoretical Propositions** <note>original: "Erwegungs-Sätze"; literally "propositions of consideration" or "weighing." These are statements that contemplate the nature of a thing.</note>; the other kind, however, we shall call **Practical Propositions** <note>original: "Ubungs-Sätze"; literally "propositions of practice" or "exercise," describing an action to be performed.</note>.

<margin>What Foundational Principles and Postulates are.</margin>
§. 13. The **Theoretical Propositions**, which are derived from a single definition <note>original: "Erklärung"; in this context, it refers to a formal logical definition.</note>, I call **Foundational Principles** <note>original: "Grund-Sätze"; often referred to in logic and geometry as axioms—truths so simple they are the "ground" for others.</note>; the **Practical Propositions**, which one concludes from a single definition, I call **Postulates** <note>original: "Heische-Sätze"; from the word "heischen," meaning to demand or request. These are the basic "tasks" required by a definition.</note>. Examples will appear below in the sixth chapter.

<margin>What Theorems and Problems are.</margin>
§. 14. The **Theoretical Propositions**, which are derived from many definitions taken together, are called **Theorems** <note>original: "Lehr-Sätze"; literally "teaching-propositions." These are more complex statements that must be proven using multiple steps.</note>: the **Practical Propositions**, which one concludes from many definitions, are called **Problems** <note>original: "Aufgaben"; literally "tasks" or "assignments." These are complex practical goals that require several steps to achieve.</note>. Examples of these will also appear in the sixth chapter, and examples of theorems have already been given above (§. 4. 5. 7.) and of a problem (§. 12.).

<margin>Utility of this classification.</margin>
§. 15. In mathematics, this classification of propositions is observed with the utmost precision, and a proper name is prescribed for each one, so that one can soon see what needs to be attended to when one wishes to contemplate any given proposition. However, this is also the basis of the rules for how one derives the propositions from their definitions—

### The 4th Chapter.

...virtuous. Now I find through experience: *Titius* aligns his actions according to the law of nature. And thus I conclude: *Titius* is virtuous. In the same way, people have sought to apply general propositions to specific cases that arise. *E.g.* <note>original: "Z. E." for "Zum Exempel."</note> One knows from experience: Whatever is warm expands the air. Now one finds that the sun's rays are warm. Thus one concludes: The sun's rays expand the air. I know that *Titius* has a good livelihood and yet I hear him seriously complaining. Therefore, I conclude: He is greedy.

<margin>The basis of these inferences.</margin>
§. 2. This way of inferring is exceedingly clear, for it all depends on this: whatever pertains to all things of a certain kind must also pertain to this specific thing, which is of that same kind. *E.g.* If I have once accepted that everything that makes surrounding things visible shall have the name "light"; then I must necessarily also call the moon, which does this very thing, a light. Namely, being a light and making everything around oneself visible is one and the same thing. Whatever I can say of the one, I must also say of the other—

<margin>The basis of these inferences.</margin> §. 4. This type of inference is also as clear as the previous one, for it depends on this: whatever has been denied of an entire kind; this very thing must also be denied of every individual of that same kind. <margin>The nature of inferences.</margin>
§. 6. If we now consider these inferences, or the manner of deriving one from another, more circumstantially; we find that each consists of three propositions <term>Sätze</term> and these propositions are composed of three members <term>Glieder</term> <note>In modern logic, these are called 'terms'.</note> in such a way that they always stand two by two next to each other. For example, our first inference was (§. 1.):

->Everything that makes surrounding things<-
->visible, is a light.<-
->The moon makes surrounding<-
->things visible.<-
->Therefore, the moon is a light.<-

Here the three propositions lie clearly before our eyes, and within them are also shown the three members of which every proposition consists. For the first member is *making surrounding things visible*; the second is *the light*; the third is *the moon*. And one finds it exactly the same in the remaining examples of inferences that we gave previously (§. 1. 3.). So that we may distinguish these three propositions from one another, we shall call the first the **Top-Proposition** <term>Major Premise</term>, the second the **Bottom-Proposition** <term>Minor Premise</term>, and the third the **Conclusion** <note>original: "Hinter-Satz," literally the "Rear-Proposition" or "Final-Sentence".</note>; the Top- and Bottom-Propositions together, however, we shall call the **Fore-Propositions** <term>Premises</term>. Likewise, the first member in a proposition shall be the **Fore-Member** <term>Subject</term>, but the other the **Rear-Member** <term>Predicate</term> (§. 3. c. 3.), and finally...

### Chapter 4.

...finally the third member in the inference shall be called the **Middle-Member** <term>Middle Term</term>.

<margin>Order of the members.</margin>
§. 7. From what has been mentioned thus far, it is clear: the **Middle-Member** can never enter into the **Conclusion** <note>original: "Hinter-Satz"</note>. On the other hand, in the **Major Premise** <term>Major Premise</term> <note>original: "Ober-Satze"</note> it is joined with the **Predicate** <note>original: "Hinter-Gliede"</note> and in the **Minor Premise** <term>Minor Premise</term> <note>original: "Unter-Satze"</note> with the **Subject** <note>original: "Förder-Gliede"</note> of the Conclusion. Likewise, it is clear that the Conclusion is inferred from the **Premises** <term>Premises</term> <note>original: "Förder-Sätzen"</note>.

<margin>Origin of the figures of inferences</margin>
§. 8. It has now been observed that the Middle-Member can be placed in various ways: namely, either once in the place of the Subject in the Major Premise and in the place of the Predicate in the Minor Premise, as in our [previous] example; or in the place of the Predicate [in both]; or even in the place of the Subject in both Premises. An example of the second case is:

->All wise people look toward the future.<-
->Some chatterboxes do not look toward the future.<-
->Therefore, some chatterboxes are not wise.<-

An example of the third case is the following:

->No fool looks toward the future.<-
->Several fools are rich.<-
->Therefore, several rich people do not look toward the future.<-

# Of Inferences, and how we etc.

Therefore, three figures of inferences have arisen—the first, the second, and the third—whose nature is to be understood from what has preceded.

<margin>Origin of the types of inferences.</margin>
§. 9. However, since propositions either universally or particularly negate or affirm something (§. 3. 5. c. 3.), and therefore there are four kinds of them; thus, every figure has different types of inferences.

<margin>Rules of inferences in general.</margin>
§. 10. So that the nature of the propositions in each type may be clear, we must first conceive that it is impossible for both **Premises** <term>Premises</term> <note>original: "Förder-Sätze," literally "forward-propositions"</note> to simultaneously affirm or negate something in particular (§. 2. 4.); likewise, both Premises cannot simultaneously negate something (§. 4.); and thus, in every inference, one of the Premises must affirm something. For one either attributes to some things of a kind what belongs to all of that kind (§. 2.), or one negates from some things of a kind what belongs to all things of that kind (§. 4.). And from this very fact it is clear that, if the **Conclusion** <note>original: "Hinter-Satz," literally "back-proposition"</note> affirms something, both Premises must likewise affirm something, and therefore only one of the Premises can negate something if the Conclusion negates.

<meta>catchword: §. 11</meta>

### Chapter 4.

<margin>
An essential
observation.
</margin>
§. 11. However, it should be well noted that the **minor premise** <term>minor premise</term> <note>original: "Unter-Satz"</note> in an inference can sometimes have the appearance of negating something, when in fact it is to be regarded as equivalent to an **affirming** one. For example, if I were to conclude:

-> No one who does not look to the future is wise. <-
-> Chatterers <note>original: "Plauderer," referring to idle talkers or those who speak without thought.</note> do not look to the future. <-
-> Therefore, chatterers are not wise ; <-

here it appears as if the minor premise—*chatterers do not look to the future*—negates something. However, because the negation arises from the **subject** <term>subject</term> <note>original: "Förder-Gliede," literally the "forward-member" of a proposition.</note> of the **major premise** <term>major premise</term> <note>original: "Ober-Satz"</note>; it is to be considered equal to an affirming proposition: For it is exactly the same as if I said:

-> Whoever is a person who does not look to <-
-> the future, that person is not wise. <-
-> Chatterers are persons who do <-
-> not look to the future. <-
-> Therefore, chatterers are not wise. <-

In such a manner, "not looking to the future" is assumed to be a characteristic of those who are not wise, and in the minor premise I affirm that this characteristic is found among the chatterers (§. 4.).

Rules
of
Inferences
</margin>
§. 12. The subject of the **conclusion** <term>conclusion</term> <note>original: "Hinter-Satz," literally the "back-proposition."</note> is simultaneously the subject of the minor

<summary>This page explains a logical nuance where a premise containing a negation can technically function as an affirmative statement if the negation is part of the defined characteristic being attributed to a subject. It uses the example of "chatterers" to show how qualities are assigned within a syllogism.</summary>

<header>Of Inferences, and how we etc.</header>

<margin>In the first figure.</margin>
**minor premise** <term>minor premise</term> <note>original: "Unter-Satz"</note> in the **first figure** <term>first figure</term> <note>original: "erste Figur," a standard classification in classical logic where the middle term is the subject of the major premise and the predicate of the minor premise.</note> (§. 8.). Since in the minor premise [the subject] cannot be linked with the **middle term** <term>middle term</term> <note>original: "Mittel-Glied"</note> in any other way than how it is found in the **conclusion** <term>conclusion</term> <note>original: "Hinter-Satz," literally the "back-proposition"</note> (§. 2. 4.); the minor premise must be a **particular** <note>original: "besonderer," referring to a statement about "some" rather than "all."</note> proposition if the conclusion is of such a kind. And consequently, in the first figure, the **major premise** <term>major premise</term> <note>original: "Ober-Satz"</note> is always **universal** <note>original: "allgemein," referring to a statement about "all" or "none."</note> (§. 10.). It is also clear that in the first figure, the minor premise must always **affirm** <note>original: "bekräfftigen"</note> something: For it states that a thing belongs under that category of which something is negated in the major premise.

<margin>Four types of inferences in the first figure.</margin>
§. 14. In such a manner, in the first figure, either both **premises** <term>premises</term> <note>original: "Förder-Sätze," literally the "forward-propositions."</note> will universally affirm something, and thus the conclusion is also universal and affirms something: or the major premise negates while the minor premise affirms something universally, and thus the conclusion must universally negate something: or the major premise affirms something universally and the minor premise does so **particularly** <note>original: "ins besondere"</note>, and then the conclusion must affirm something particularly: or the major premise negates something universally and the minor premise affirms something particularly, and so the conclusion must negate something particularly (§. 10. 11.). Therefore, there are four types of inferences in the first figure.

<sig>F 3</sig>
<meta>§. 14.</meta>

<summary>This page defines the specific rules for the 'first figure' of logical syllogisms, explaining how universal and particular premises interact to produce valid conclusions. It concludes that there are exactly four valid modes of reasoning within this specific logical structure.</summary>
<keywords>syllogism, first figure, major premise, minor premise, universal proposition, particular proposition, logical inference, deduction</keywords>

# Chapter 4.

<margin>The first figure is sufficient for all conclusions.</margin>

§. 14. Since no other **conclusion** <term>conclusion</term> <note>original: "Hinter-Satz," literally the "back-sentence" or the final proposition in a logical argument.</note> can occur except one that either affirms or denies something, either universally or particularly (§. 3. 4. c. 3), and since these four types of conclusions are found within the varieties of **inferences** <term>inferences</term> <note>original: "Schlüsse"</note> of the **first figure** <term>first figure</term> <note>original: "erste Figur," the most direct arrangement of a logical argument where the middle term is the subject of the first premise and the predicate of the second.</note> (§. 13); therefore, all inferences can take place within the first figure. And, because I am not inclined to delay beginners with superfluous matters—especially with those things that are made so hateful to them nowadays <note>The author likely refers to the overly complex and "dry" scholastic logic taught in traditional universities of the time, which many students found tedious.</note>—I will omit the second and third figures entirely. For if someone encounters an inference in the second or third figure, he can easily create an inference in the first figure from the conclusion and the **middle term** <term>middle term</term> <note>original: "Mittel-Glied," the shared concept that connects the two premises.</note>, which concludes much more naturally. For example:

Above, we conclude in the second figure:
->All wise people look to the future.<-
->Some chatterboxes do not look to the future.<-
->Therefore, some chatterboxes are not wise.<-

This inference is immediately brought into the first figure if we simply make the middle term the **subject** <term>subject term</term> <note>original: "Förder-Glied," literally the "forward-member," referring to the term that comes first in a logical proposition.</note> of the **major premise** <term>major premise</term> <note>original: "Ober-Satz"</note>, as follows:
->No one who does not look to the future is wise.<-
->Some chatterboxes do not look to the future.<-
->Therefore, some chatterboxes are not wise.<-

§. 15.

<summary>The author explains that the "first figure" of logical syllogisms is sufficient for all reasoning, allowing him to bypass the more complex second and third figures. He demonstrates how an argument can be converted into the first figure to make it more intuitive for the reader.</summary>

# On Syllogisms, and how we etc.

<margin>
In the
first
figure.
</margin>
§. 15. However, so that beginners might see more clearly by name in what manner and in what way a **syllogism** <term>syllogism</term> <note>original: "Schluß," the formal logical structure where a conclusion is drawn from two premises.</note> must be constructed when the **conclusion** <term>conclusion</term> <note>original: "Hinter-Satz"</note> and the **middle term** <term>middle term</term> <note>original: "Mittel-Glied"</note> are given, they have designated each type with an essential name. From these names, the nature of the propositions in each type of syllogism can be seen immediately. Namely, they have named the four types of the first figure **BArBArA, CElArEnt, DArII, FErIO**. In these words, the **A** signifies a **universal affirmative** <term>universal affirmative</term> <note>A statement that applies to everything in a category, e.g., "All humans are mortal."</note> proposition; the **E** a **universal negative** <term>universal negative</term> <note>A statement that excludes everything in a category, e.g., "No circles are squares."</note>; the **I** a **particular affirmative** <term>particular affirmative</term> <note>A statement about some members of a category, e.g., "Some humans are wise."</note>; and finally, the **O** signifies a **particular negative** <term>particular negative</term> <note>A statement excluding some members of a category, e.g., "Some humans are not wise."</note> proposition. To remember this better, they have composed the following verses:

> **A affirms, E denies; but both universally.** <note>original: "Afferit A, negat E; sed universaliter ambæ."</note>
> **I affirms, O denies; but both particularly.** <note>original: "Afferit I, negat O; sed particulariter ambæ."</note>

**bA All scholars are men.**
**rA Therefore, all scholars must die.**

# Chapter 4.

->II.<-

**CE** No one whose understanding has limits is omniscient.
**lA** Every human's understanding has limits.
**rEnt** Therefore, no human is omniscient.
<note>This represents the "Celarent" mood: a universal negative (E), followed by a universal affirmative (A), resulting in a universal negative conclusion (E).</note>

->III.<-

**DA** Whoever speaks well of everyone has many friends.
**rI** Some people speak well of everyone.
**I** Therefore, some people have many friends.
<note>This represents the "Darii" mood: a universal affirmative (A), followed by a particular affirmative (I), resulting in a particular affirmative conclusion (I).</note>

->IV.<-

**FE** No one who indulges their desires loves virtue.
**rI** Some people indulge their desires.
**O** Therefore, some people do not love virtue.
<note>This represents the "Ferio" mood: a universal negative (E), followed by a particular affirmative (I), resulting in a particular negative conclusion (O).</note>

<margin>Conclusions under conditions.</margin>
§. 18. Sometimes, one also makes conclusions under conditions <note>This refers to what modern logic calls hypothetical or conditional syllogisms.</note>, the total content of which results in the following manner:

If A is, then B is also.
Now A is.
Therefore, B is also.
->or :<-
If A is, then B is not.
Now A is.
Therefore, B is not.

For example: If all humans sometimes fail <note>original: "fehlen," meaning to err, sin, or make a mistake.</note>, then **Titius** <note>Titius was a traditional placeholder name used in Latin legal and logical texts, much like "John Doe" is used today.</note> can also sometimes fail.
Now, all humans can sometimes fail.
Therefore, Titius can also sometimes fail.

->Again:<-
If Titius loves virtue, he does not associate with evil company.
Now the first is true.
Therefore, the second is also true.

### The 4th Chapter.

One can, however, very easily bring these conclusions into a proper form, if one only pays attention to the **minor premise** <term>minor premise</term> <note>original: "Hinter-Satz." In formal logic, this is the second statement of an argument that links a specific case to a general rule.</note> and the **middle term** <term>middle term</term> <note>original: "Mittel-Glied." This is the concept that appears in both premises but disappears in the final conclusion, serving as the "bridge" between ideas.</note>. For example, the previously established conclusions are transformed into the following:

All humans can sometimes fail.
Titius is a human.
Therefore, Titius can also sometimes fail.

->Again:<-

Whoever loves virtue does not associate with evil company.
Titius loves virtue.
Therefore, Titius does not associate with evil company.

<margin>Another kind of conclusion.</margin>
§. 19. Sometimes one also makes conclusions of the following content:
Either this is, or that is.
Now this is.
Therefore, that is not.

->or:<-

Now this is not.
Therefore, that is.

For example: Peter is either penitent <note>original: "bußfertig," meaning feeling or showing sorrow and regret for having done wrong.</note>, or he persists in sin.
Now he is penitent.
Therefore, he does not persist in sin.

->Again:<-

Either God is unjust, or he must punish blasphemies <note>original: "Lästerungen," referring here to the act of speaking sacrilegiously about God or sacred things.</note>.
Now God is not unjust.
Therefore, he must punish blasphemies.

# Of Conclusions, and how we etc.

These types of conclusions can also be transformed into others of the **first figure** <term>first figure</term> <note>In traditional logic, the "first figure" is the most direct and "perfect" form of a syllogism, where a general rule is applied to a specific subject to reach a certain conclusion.</note>, as seen in our examples:

> Whoever is penitent <note>original: "bußfertig." To be penitent means to feel sincere regret or remorse for one’s sins or wrongdoings.</note> does not persist in sin.
> Peter is penitent.
> Therefore, he does not persist in sin.

->Again:<-

> Whoever is not unjust punishes blasphemies.
> God is not unjust.
> Therefore, he punishes blasphemies.

<margin>The utility of conclusions.</margin>
§. 20. Through these conclusions, everything is discovered that is brought forth by human reason, and all other things are proven to those who wish to be convinced of their certainty—regardless of the fact that we do not always have the formal structure of the conclusions clearly before our eyes, neither in the act of discovery nor in proving. But anyone who pays proper attention to themselves, whether they are meditating or pondering a proof, will realize this all too well. My mathematical writings as well as those I have published on the various parts of **philosophy** <term>philosophy</term> <note>original: "Welt-Weißheit," literally "world-wisdom." <margin>
The nature of a correct proof.

§. 21. However, one must not imagine that a proof can be brought to a conclusion with a single **syllogism** <term>syllogism</term> <note>In logic, a syllogism is a three-part argument consisting of two premises and a conclusion that follows necessarily from them.</note>. Because one admits the **conclusion** <term>conclusion</term> <note>original: "Hinter-Satz," literally the "after-sentence."</note> for the sake of the **premises** <term>premises</term> <note>original: "Förder-Sätze," literally the "fore-sentences."</note>; so one cannot be certain of its truth until the correctness of the premises is understood. Therefore, the premises must be tested with new syllogisms for as long as necessary: until we arrive at a syllogism where the premises are either definitions, **axioms** <term>axioms</term> <note>original: "Grund-Sätze," or "fundamental principles." These are self-evident truths that do not require further proof.</note>, correct experiences, or previously proven propositions. One need not always cite both premises; rather, one can abbreviate the syllogisms (§. 17). **Mathematicians** <note>original: "Mathematici."</note> sometimes omit both premises because one is brought to mind from the citation and the other from the **diagram** <note>original: "Figur." In geometry, the physical drawing or figure serves as one of the premises for a logical step.</note>. In this manner, a proof is a collection of abbreviated, yet orderly connected syllogisms. And one calls it a **demonstration** <term>demonstration</term>, when one can carry its syllogisms so far that in the final syllogism, one has nothing but definitions, clear experiences, and other **identical propositions** <term>identical propositions</term> <note>These are statements where the subject and predicate are the same, such as "A is A," representing the highest level of logical certainty.</note> as premises. However, it should be noted that it is not always necessary to carry the proof out to its ultimate foundations; rather, it is enough if one [does not... <note>The sentence continues on the next page.</note>]

## Chapter 4

[...at a] single point above a line, they make 180°. The figure shows: The angles I, 3, and II stand at a point C above a line DE. Therefore he concludes: The angles I, 3, and II together make 180°. > Equal angles can be substituted for equal ones without the size of the latter being changed.

The proof shows: 1 and I, as well as II and 2, are equal angles. Therefore he concludes: angle 1 can be placed in front of I, and 2 in front of II, without the size of the angles I, 3, and II being changed; consequently, he sees that 1, 2, and 3 together also make 180°. <margin>Through formal deductions, one arrives at geometric demonstrations.</margin>

The experiment provides me with this deduction:

> Whatever begins to expand when resistance is removed has an expansive force.
> The air begins to expand when resistance is removed.
> Therefore, it has an expansive force.

And so we have all the material for a mathematical proof. However, according to the guidance of the conclusions reached, the presentation must proceed as follows.

->Explanation.<-

## Corollary.

7. <margin>In discourses one should not state the syllogisms in their entirety.</margin>
§. 27. Even though I have praised the utility of formal syllogisms as is fitting; it is by no means the intention that one should set them down everywhere in sequence. ### Of Syllogisms, and how etc.

Thus, one can never speak of discretion <note>original: "Verschwiegenheit." The virtue of knowing when to keep silent.</note> other than well. For one easily sees that in the minor premise <term>minor premise</term> <note>original: "Unter-Satz." The second statement in a formal argument that links a specific case to a general rule.</note> and the conclusion <term>conclusion</term> <note>original: "Hinter-Satz." Literally the "after-sentence," the logical result of the premises.</note>, only equivalent turns of phrase are put in the place of the proper ones, and the syllogism <term>syllogism</term> <note>original: "Schluß." A three-part logical argument.</note> is in fact this:
> Virtue is to be praised.
> Discretion is a virtue.
> Therefore, discretion is to be praised.

An example of hidden syllogisms <term>hidden syllogisms</term> <note>original: "versteckten Schlüssen." Also known as enthymemes, these are arguments where parts of the logical structure are implied rather than stated out loud.</note> is also this argument:
> Virtue is a skill for promoting one's well-being.
> Temperance is a skill for promoting one's well-being.
> Therefore, temperance is a virtue.

For it is identical with this one:
> All skill for promoting one's well-being is a virtue.
> Temperance is a skill for promoting one's well-being.
> Therefore, temperance is a virtue.

There are very many kinds of hidden syllogisms that one practiced in reasoning can easily recognize if he brings them into their proper form.

§. 29. Occasionally it has the appearance as if one brings a conclusion out of a single premise <term>premise</term> <note>original: "Förder-Satz." Literally a "furthering-sentence" or antecedent.</note>:

### Chapter 4. Of Inferences <unclear>Schlüss</unclear> <note>Likely "Schlüssen," meaning inferences or syllogisms.</note>

Which is called an immediate consequence <term>immediate consequence</term> <note>original: "unmittelbahre Folge." A logical step where a conclusion is drawn from a single premise without an explicit middle term.</note>. For example, if I say:
->The triangle is a figure.<-
->Therefore, whoever describes the triangle,<-
->describes a figure;<-
it has the appearance as if I were bringing one proposition <term>proposition</term> <note>original: "Satz." A statement that can be true or false.</note> immediately out of another. However, one can easily see that it is impossible to arrive at the second proposition through the first alone, because otherwise the second would necessarily occur to us as soon as we hear the first: which, however, does not happen. In some cases of these immediate consequences, this is even more evident. For example, if I say:
->Every animal senses.<-
->Therefore, some animals sense;<-
this is in fact a truncated proposition, where the minor premise <term>minor premise</term> <note>original: "Unter-Satz."</note>, "some animals are animals," is omitted. However, so that it does not result in a mere dispute over words, it should be noted that I say one proposition follows from another when it is possible for it to occur to me as soon as I hear or even think the other. I deal with these immediate consequences more extensively in the *Thoughts on God, the World, and the Soul of Man* <note>original: "Gedancken von GOTT, der Welt un der Seele des Menschen." This refers to Christian Wolff's influential 1720 work on metaphysics, often called the "German Metaphysics."</note> (§ 354 and following <note>original: "seqq." An abbreviation for the Latin *sequentia*, meaning "and the following sections."</note>).

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>The author defines "immediate consequences" in logic, arguing that they are actually hidden syllogisms where a premise—such as "some animals are animals"—has been left out. He emphasizes that a logical consequence is valid when one thought naturally and necessarily triggers the next.</summary>

# On Experience, etc.

### Chapter 5

## On Experience, and how propositions are found through it.

->§. 1.<-

<margin>What we experience.</margin>
We experience everything that we recognize when we pay attention to our sensations <term>sensations</term> <note>original: "Empfindungen." In this context, it refers to the raw data provided by our senses before it is processed by reason.</note>. For example: I see that when a candle is lit, everything around me becomes visible; that poured water makes the table wet; that the flame of the candle ignites the paper, and so on. All such knowledge <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "Erkenntnis." Often translated as "cognition" in philosophical texts, referring to the act or result of knowing.</note> is called experience.

->§. 2.<-

<margin>The duty of one who appeals to experience.</margin>
Since we perceive nothing but individual things, experiences are also nothing but propositions <term>propositions</term> <note>original: "Sätze." In logic, a statement that affirms or denies something and can be judged as true or false.</note> about individual things. Therefore, anyone who appeals to experience is fairly required to cite a specific case, unless the experience is of such a nature that anyone can have it immediately if they so desire, or at least can quickly recall it because they have had it themselves more than once before. ### Chapter 5: On Experience

# and how through this the propositions etc.

I could cite a case of this. For because it is a matter that occurs daily; so there is likely no one who should not have had this experience many times. How experiences are to be distinguished from the propositions <term>propositions</term> <note>original: "Sätze." Logical claims or statements derived from data.</note> which are concluded from them.
</margin>

§. 4. It is, however, to be gathered from the cited examples that not everyone distinguishes the propositions which they concluded from experience from the experience itself, but quite often passes the former off as the latter. And those who contradict one another, and both appeal to experience, do not oppose their experiences, but rather their propositions, which they have drawn from them, against one another. This, however, should rightly not happen. For that one had such or other sensations <term>sensations</term> <note>original: "Empfindungen." The raw data our senses pick up before we think about them.</note>, he cannot prove except through witnesses, if some were present, and thus he rightly demands that one should believe him. However, he cannot demand that I believe he has concluded correctly, for this can be judged according to the rules of reasoning <term>reasoning</term> <note>original: "vernünfftig zu schliessen." Literally "to close/conclude rationally."</note>, because nothing is more common than that one seeks to surreptitiously obtain propositions <note>original: "Sätze zu erschleichen." A logical fallacy where one sneaks in a conclusion that isn't actually proven by the evidence.</note> through experience: of which I have given an example in the Metaphysics <note>Likely referring to Christian Wolff’s "Vernünftige Gedanken von Gott, der Welt und der Seele des Menschen," commonly known as his German Metaphysics, first published in 1719.</note> (§. 529.).

<meta>catchword: §. 5.</meta>

<summary>This page distinguishes between "experience" (the raw sensory data) and "propositions" (the logical conclusions drawn from that data). The author argues that scientific disagreements usually arise from faulty reasoning rather than false sensations, and that while we must trust a witness's sensations, we must critically evaluate their logic.</summary>

Various cases of experiences.

§. 5. We perceive either the things themselves with their properties <term>properties</term> <note>original: "Eigenschafften." In the first case, we form the concepts of the things that we perceive, as has already been shown above (Chapter 1, Section 5, and following <note>original: "& seqq." abbreviation for the Latin "et sequentia," meaning "and the following pages/sections."</note>), or also propositions <term>propositions</term> <note>original: "Sätze." ...can correctly determine this. This, however, occurs in many ways. First, it must be repeated here what was already presented above in the definitions of terms (§ 42, ch. 1 <note>original: "c. 1." Abbreviation for "caput," Latin for "chapter."</note>). Namely, it is sometimes within our power to remove the things whose properties <term>properties</term> <note>original: "Eigenschafften." Characteristics that belong to the nature of an object.</note> we wish to recognize from the other things surrounding them, and move them to another place where they are surrounded by entirely different things than before. 
<margin><unclear>no. 15</unclear></margin>
From this, one can determine whether the reason why a trait belongs to a thing is to be sought outside of it or not. For example: I find wax lying in the sun or otherwise in the heat, and it is soft. I assume that I do not yet know whether wax is always soft or not. So, I take it and place it in a cold spot, perhaps a cellar, and find that it becomes hard there. At this, I recall that something a thing does not constantly retain is not a property, and from this I conclude (§ 16, ch. 4) that softness is not an inherent property of wax. If it is soft, the cause is to be sought in other things surrounding it—in our case, the sun's rays shining upon it. On the other hand, I may take a stone wherever I wish; it remains hard. Since it now [retains] hardness constantly...

<margin>The second way.</margin>
§ 7. If we are not permitted to move the object to another location, we simply observe whether it still possesses the same trait at a different time when it is under different circumstances. For example: someone sees that the rising and setting sun is oval. But if he waits until it rises further above the **horizon**, the oval shape disappears and it appears consistently round. From this, I conclude that the oval shape the sun has at the horizon is not inherent to it; for at the very same time that it is at the horizon for us, it is standing directly overhead in another location. Furthermore, when it comes near our <term>zenith</term> <note>original: "Scheitel." Literally the crown of the head, referring to the highest point in the sky directly above an observer.</note>, it is seen at the horizon in another place, as is sufficiently clear from **geography**. It is exactly the same when I find wax soft in the heat, yet find it hard at another time in the cold.

<margin>When one experiences the causes of changes.</margin>
§ 6. When one thing causes a change in another, and the change occurs as soon as the one comes to the other, or one body is mixed with the other, there is no doubt that the one is the cause of the change in the other. §. 10. In order to become certain whether one thing alone is the cause of a change in another, or not; one must diligently investigate whether perhaps either one of the two, or even both together, had already undergone a change beforehand, without which the other would never have occurred: indeed, whether something else entirely might even contribute to the change. Thus, an unusual change in the water occurred here because it had already undergone some change beforehand from the particles of iron filings—dissolved by the oil of vitriol—which had attached themselves to the glass. ### Chapter 5: On Experience

In this way, one will find whether something has been listed as a cause of an effect that is not actually necessary for it, and at the same time recognize what each part contributes to the matter. For example: Gunpowder is made from sulfur, charcoal, and saltpeter. Now, if I want to learn whether these three things are all necessary for gunpowder, and what each of them contributes to the effect of the gunpowder: one must first investigate how sulfur, charcoal, and saltpeter behave individually when exposed to fire. After this, I mix sulfur and charcoal, sulfur and saltpeter, and saltpeter and charcoal with one another and observe what these mixtures are capable of. Finally, I take all three together and find—as I have shown in my *Principles of Artillery* <note>original: "Anfangs-Gründen von der Artillerie"; likely referring to the author's work on the mathematical and physical foundations of ballistics and explosives.</note>—that all three things are necessarily required for gunpowder, and what each contributes to the effect of the gunpowder; namely, the charcoal catches the fire and melts the sulfur and saltpeter, the sulfur vapor allows the saltpeter to ignite into a flame, and the saltpeter serves to cause the violent expansion of the ignited powder.

## Chapter 6.

# On the Discovery of Propositions from Definitions and on the Solution of Problems.

->§ 1.<-

<margin>
How axioms and postulates are found.
</margin>
**Axioms** <term>Grund-Sätze</term> <note>Foundational principles that are accepted as true without needing further proof.</note> and **postulates** <term>Heische-Sätze</term> <note>Literally "demand-propositions"; these are requirements or assumptions needed to perform a task or proceed with an argument.</note> are derived from a **definition** <note>original: "Erklärung"; in this logical context, it refers to the formal statement of a thing's essence.</note> (§ 13, ch. 3). Therefore, nothing more is required here than that we strive to seek **clear concepts** of that which is contained in the definitions, and to consider them both in themselves and in relation to one another. For example: If I take the definition of **joy**, stating that it is an **emotion** <note>original: "Affect"; a strong feeling or mental state.</note> that arises within us when we believe ourselves to be **convinced** <note>original: "überführet"; the state of being fully persuaded or certain of a fact.</note> of a present good, then we can examine the concept of the "present good" and the "supposed conviction." Since I now see from the definition, for example, that whoever learns to realize that he is not yet convinced, his joy will be disturbed; and in reflection find,

whoever thinks himself convinced and is not, can recognize that he is far from being convinced; thus I immediately conclude from the definition (§ 16, ch. 4): Whoever thinks himself convinced of a present good and is not, his joy can be disturbed. In this manner, the following **axiom** <term>Grund-Satz</term> <note>A self-evident principle that serves as a basis for reasoning.</note> is clear: Whoever is not actually convinced of a present good, his joy can be disturbed. Likewise, this **postulate** <term>Heiſche-Satz</term> <note>A requirement or a proposition that demands a certain action or possibility; here, the possibility of interrupting someone's joy.</note> is clear: If one makes someone who is not actually convinced of a present good doubtful, then one can interrupt his joy.

<margin>Why these propositions require no proof</margin>
§ 2. Since everyone can quickly recognize the correctness of these propositions if they reflect on the definitions, it is not necessary to first provide a proof for them. Consequently, it is common to say of them: They require no proof; they are clear in themselves—namely, as soon as one understands the definition from which they are drawn by means of an **inference** <term>Schluss</term> <note>A logical conclusion or syllogism.</note>. Otherwise, it cannot be denied that if one wished to proceed with absolute rigor, a proof could be added, though it would not be an extensive one. However, if one insists on being overly precise, it becomes burdensome to those who are not particularly [adept in such proofs]

<margin>
How the
propositions
are discovered.
</margin>
§ 3. Propositions <term>Lehr-Säze</term> <note>Formal statements or theorems that express a truth requiring demonstration.</note> are discovered when one compares various definitions <term>Erklärungen</term> with one another (§ 14, ch. 3). This occurs when one seeks clear concepts <term>Begriffe</term> for everything contained in the definitions, and also methodically represents everything one has either recognized through experience <term>Erfahrung</term> regarding these things whose definitions are given, or what has been previously proven about them. In this way, it will soon become apparent what new conclusions can be inferred from them. For example: Joy <term>Freude</term> is an affect <term>Affect</term> <note>In 18th-century philosophy, an "affect" refers to a strong emotion or a state of being moved by an external influence.</note> which is stirred within us when we believe ourselves to be convinced of a present good <term>Gut</term>. Sadness <term>Traurigkeit</term>, however, is an affect which manifests in us when we believe ourselves to be convinced of a present evil <term>Übel</term>. From the definition of joy, I see: Whoever feels joy does not necessarily have to be actually convinced of the present good. It occurs to me then: Whoever is not actually convinced of the present good can take evil for good. And thus I conclude (§ 16, ch. 4): Whoever feels joy can regard the present evil as good. As it further occurs to me: Whoever views the present evil as good, can [be] convic[ted]...

### Chapter 6: On the Discovery of Propositions

...be convinced that what he holds to be good is an evil; thus I further conclude: Whoever feels joy can be convinced that what he holds to be good is an evil. I consider further: Whoever is convinced that what he holds to be good is an evil becomes sad, by virtue of the definition of sadness. And from this I conclude: Whoever feels joy can become sad. In such a way, this proposition <term>Lehr-Satz</term> <note>A formal statement or theorem that expresses a truth requiring demonstration.</note> reveals itself: If one can convince someone who is rejoicing in something that what he considers good should instead be regarded as an evil, then his joy will be turned into sadness. It is not necessary, however, that when one wishes to find propositions, one always begins with definitions: for one may simply adopt propositions that have previously been derived either from definitions or from experience. In all the parts of philosophy <term>Welt-Weisheit</term> <note>Literally "world-wisdom," the standard 18th-century German term for philosophy.</note> that I have written, I derive most propositions in such a manner. And anyone who examines mathematical writings—especially my Latin *Foundations of all Mathematical Sciences* <note>original: "Anfangs-Gründe der gesammten mathematischen Wissenschafften," referring to Wolff’s *Elementa matheseos universae*.</note>—in the manner I directed in the *Method of Lecturing* <note>original: "Ratione Prælectionum," specifically section 1, chapter 2, paragraph 38.</note>, will find that there, too, the propositions can be derived from one another in exactly this way.

<header>from the explanations, and of etc.</header>

<margin>How one attains the ability to discover propositions.</margin>
->§. 4.<-
Whoever intends to reach a facility in discovering propositions <term>Lehr-Sätze</term> <note>A "Lehr-Satz" is a formal statement or theorem that expresses a truth requiring logical demonstration.</note> must begin with such definitions <term>Erklärungen</term> <note>Wolff uses "Erklärungen" (explanations) to mean formal definitions that lay the groundwork for logic.</note> that contain very little within them, and from which flow such propositions whose correctness can be tested quickly. For if the definitions contain little, they are much easier to reflect upon than if they were densely packed. And if one can test the correctness of the discovered propositions quickly, it becomes clear whether we have made correct conclusions in our reflections or not. Now, we find no definitions to which these required properties are better suited than those in arithmetic <term>Rechen-Kunst</term> and geometry. Therefore, anyone who intends to practice the art of discovery should rightly begin with these two disciplines. Should someone wish to go through my Latin Mathematics <note>original: "Lateinische Mathematick," referring to Wolff’s multi-volume *Elementa matheseos universae*.</note> in the manner previously directed (§. 3) and then add the writings I have published concerning philosophy <term>Welt-Weisheit</term> <note>Literally "world-wisdom," the standard 18th-century German term for philosophy.</note>, he would progress further in this exercise than he likely first imagined. However, everything requires time, and one must not seek to rush.

# Chapter 6: On the Discovery of Propositions

...an effect <term>Würckung</term> <note>In 18th-century logic, an "effect" often refers to the goal or the specific proposition one is trying to prove or bring about.</note> is presupposed as known. We arrive at this knowledge in many ways. For either we assume an effect is possible following the guidance of other thoughts, even though we do not yet know for certain whether it is possible or not, but first want to find it out: or we are also accustomed at times to derive an effect from other known truths: or we perceive it through experience.

<margin>
First way to solve the problems.
</margin>

§. 6. When one assumes an effect at will, one must examine its concept <term>Begriff</term> so that one becomes aware of what is required for it. Then one must reflect on everything that is known to us of such things, so that we see whether something occurs to us from which this effect can arise. For example: one assumes in ethics <term>Sitten-Lehre</term> <note>Literally "teaching of customs," the standard term for moral philosophy or ethics at the time.</note> that trust in God is to be stirred in a person. If I now examine what trust in God is, I find that it arises from a conviction <term>Überführung</term> <note>Here, "Überführung" means a rational or logical conviction—being fully "convinced" by evidence or argument.</note> that God knows how to help, that He can help, and also wants to help. Therefore, I reflect on that which I know of God, and find among other things that He is omniscient and wise, powerful and kind. Here it occurs to me: Whoever is omniscient and all-wise,

is one who knows how to help; He who is almighty is one who can help; He who is kind is one who is willing to help. And thus I conclude from this (§. 16, ch. 4) that **God** knows how to help, can help, and is willing to help. From this, I see without effort: If one wishes to lead someone to a trust in **God**, one must impart to them a distinct concept <term>deutlicher Begriff</term> <note>In 18th-century philosophy, a "distinct concept" is a high level of understanding where one can clearly list the individual characteristics that make up an idea.</note> of **God's** omniscience, wisdom, power, and goodness, and convince <term>überführen</term> them of these attributes. In ethics and politics, very many problems <term>Aufgaben</term> are solved in this manner.

§. 7. Mathematical problems can also be included here, since one is required to find other values from certain arbitrarily assumed numbers, or even points and lines. In these cases, one examines the distinct concepts of the given numbers, and of the nature of the given points and lines, or also carefully reflects upon what else has been distinctly recognized regarding them. For in this way, either the solution will present itself, or we will find that it is not within our power. For example: I wish to know how I can find the numbers themselves from the sum of two numbers and their difference. Here I consider that the sum arises when the two numbers are added together; their difference

Therefore, one must begin with the easiest tasks <term>Aufgaben</term> and progress toward the difficult ones, but in the latter, one must see if one might use the previous ones to reach a solution <term>Auflösung</term>. For it is precisely in this that this method of reflection differs from the algebraic one: in algebra, one views the tasks in isolation from others, or at least connected to only a few truths; here, however, one must derive the solution through a connection with other truths. For this reason, one can progress more quickly using the algebraic method. The ancients <note>The author refers to classical Greek mathematicians, who primarily used geometric construction and logical deduction rather than symbolic algebra.</note> followed the other path, which I describe here, and thus they were not able to take things as far as the modern geometers <term>Geometræ</term> <note>original: "Geometræ"</note>.

And this is even less surprising because any task can be effortlessly transformed into a proposition. For if you make the solution the condition <note>The "if" part of a logical statement.</note>, and the statement the conclusion <note>The "then" part of a logical statement.</note>, you then have a proposition. # Chapter 6: On the Invention of Propositions
<meta>catchword: §. 10,</meta>

Take the example given just a moment ago (§ 7): if one says that when the difference is subtracted from the sum and the remainder is divided by 2, the smaller number results—in this way, the task <term>Aufgabe</term> <note>A "task" or "problem" in this context refers to a specific question or goal one sets out to achieve or calculate.</note> is transformed into a proposition <term>Lehr-Satz</term>. In Ethics <term>Moral</term> <note>original: "Moral." In the 18th century, this referred to the formal, philosophical study of human conduct and duties.</note> and Politics, I have produced the solution to many—indeed, likely most—tasks in such a manner. For example: this includes how the will is improved (§ 373, Ethics), which I derive from the nature of the will; how a person should conduct themselves regarding eating and drinking, which is derived from other previously proven grounds (§ 484, Ethics); and how one should teach children to handle money, which was discovered through several grounds established in Ethics (§ 110, Politics). Indeed, the general task by which I discovered what is taught concerning the upbringing of children and the organization of the commonwealth <term>Einrichtung des gemeinen Wesens</term> in Politics (<note>original: "c. 3. part. 1. & c. 3. part. 2."</note> chapter 3, part 1, and chapter 3, part 2), I have solved in this manner.

Cases of the third kind.
</margin>
§ 9. When someone recognizes an effect of nature or art through experience <term>Erfahrung</term>; he is either present when it occurs and pays attention to it; or he is present and does not pay attention to it; or he is absent.

<margin>
How one recognizes the causes of an effect <term>Würckung</term>.
</margin>

§ 10. In the first case, one recognizes all causes that contribute something to an effect <term>Würckung</term> <note>In 18th-century philosophy, "effect" refers to any change or result produced by a cause.</note> if they all fall within the senses <term>Sinnen</term> <note>Meaning they are perceptible to sight, touch, etc.</note>. He recognizes some of them if only some fall within the senses. He recognizes none at all if none fall within the senses. 

For example: If a bladder <term>Blase</term> <note>In historical physics experiments, animal bladders were commonly used as we use rubber balloons today to study air pressure and heat.</note> that is only slightly inflated but tied shut tightly at the top is held over a coal fire <term>Kohl-Feuer</term>, it inflates so forcefully that it eventually bursts. Here, I perceive all the causes of these strange effects with my own eyes—namely, the coal fire and the air <term>Lufft</term> trapped within the bladder. 

On the other hand, if I hang a needle on the south pole <term>Süderpol</term> of a magnet and bring the south pole of another magnet near it, the needle runs away from it just as if it were being blown away by force, yet I see nothing that drives it forward. 

If we cannot grasp any cause at all with our senses—either because they do not fall within our perception, or because we are not paying attention, or because we are not even present—then we must carefully consider how the matter was constituted before it underwent the change <term>Veränderung</term>, and how it is constituted now that it has undergone it. For from this, one can deduce what is required for the given change, and thus at least this much is known: it mus-

### Chapter 6: On the Discovery of Propositions

...must have been such causes that were capable of doing this or that. If we now go through everything known to us and encounter such things as are capable of doing the like, and can also prove that the change could have originated from them alone—at least in the present case—then we have not only discovered the causes but also what they contribute to the given change, and in such a manner we have fully resolved the task.

*e.g.* <note>Original: "Z. E." abbreviation for "Zum Exempel."</note> I see that an iron needle initially hangs straight down from the *magnet*, as *gravity* <term>Schwere</term> <note>In the 18th century, "Schwere" referred to the inherent weight of an object or the force of gravity pulling it downward.</note> requires. But as soon as I approach it with *another magnet*, it begins to rise and maintain a position *inclined* <term>inclinirten</term> toward the *horizon*. Here I soon realize that something is necessary which lifts the needle and keeps it suspended in the air so that it cannot fall back. 

Since, however, no solid body <term>Cörper</term> <note>Referring to a physical, tangible object.</note> is present to which this performance could be attributed, it must be a flowing stream <term>fliessender Strom</term> <note>This reflects the "effluvium" theory of the Enlightenment, which proposed that invisible, fluid-like particles flowed from magnets to exert physical force.</note> that lifts the needle and does not let it fall back, just as we recall that if we let a solid body hang from a string into a river, the current carries it along as far as the string allows. # Chapter 6: On the Discovery of Propositions #

<margin>
How to determine what each cause contributes.
</margin>

§ 11. After the causes have become known, it can further be determined what each of them contributes to a given *effect* <term>Würckung</term>, if one partly examines their clear concepts and partly reflects on everything else known about them, no differently than when one is meant to find *theorems* <term>Lehr-Sätze</term> (§ 3). For example: if a glass with a narrow neck, which is not completely filled with water but still has some air at the top, is quickly turned upside down; initially a few drops of water will run out, but after that, not even the smallest amount will follow. One must now find how this occurs. The causes are the enclosed air above the water, the water itself, the narrow opening of the vessel, and the *outermost air* <note>The surrounding atmosphere or ambient air pressure.</note>. Regarding the enclosed air, I recall that its *expansive force* <term>ausdehnende Krafft</term> <note>A term used in early physics to describe what we now call air pressure or elasticity.</note> can balance the *gravity* <term>Schweere</term> <note>In this context, the author refers to the weight or pressure of the atmospheric column.</note> of the entire air; if it receives free space, it expands further, and, in the process of expanding, its expansive force is reduced.

<meta>catchword: From</meta>

### from the explanations, and of etc. ###

Regarding the **external air**, I am aware that it cannot penetrate into the vessel and let the liquid matter out at the same time through a narrow opening: as is sufficiently experienced when one wishes to let drops fall from a full **drop-glass** <note>A "Tropffen-Glas" is a small glass container with a very narrow opening, similar to a modern pipette or dropper, used to dispense liquids in small, controlled amounts.</note>. Furthermore, it occurs to me regarding the **external air** that it can support water in an empty tube up to **32 feet** high <note>This refers to the "Torricellian vacuum" experiment; early scientists discovered that atmospheric pressure can only support a column of water up to about 10 meters (roughly 32 to 34 feet) before a vacuum forms at the top.</note>.

If I now hold these things against each other, I find that the **expansive force** <term>ausdehnende Krafft</term> of the enclosed air and the **weight** <term>Schweere</term> <note>While "Schweere" literally means "heaviness" or "gravity," in this context it refers to the pressure exerted by the weight of the fluid or the atmosphere.</note> of the water taken together is greater than the **weight** of the external air; consequently, the water must fall. However, as a few drops flow out, the enclosed air becomes thinner <note>The air expands to fill the space left by the departing water, thus becoming less dense.</note>. Since its **expansive force** decreases in this case, I find that just enough water must remain behind until its **weight**, combined with the now-reduced **expansive force**, equals the **weight** of the external air.

And because the water cannot exit and the air enter through the narrow opening at the same time, it is impossible for even a single further drop to flow out. It would be too tedious if I were to set down everything here in order, as one thought follows another in systematic reflection. However, when this happens, the use of **formal conclusions**—

# Chapter 6: On the Discovery of Propositions #

...express conclusions <term>Schlüsse</term> <note>In the logic of this period, a "Schluß" is a formal deduction or syllogism, the process of moving from known facts to a new piece of knowledge.</note> clearly. Although it should be noted here (which I shall explain more clearly in another place) that, just as our concepts <term>Begriffe</term> are often indistinct, a similar indistinctness <term>Undeutlichkeit</term> is found in our conclusions. Consequently, most people do not always perceive these logical steps and therefore do not consider an indistinct conclusion to be a formal, valid deduction. Anyone who desires more experiments <term>Versuche</term> <note>The author uses "Versuche" to mean both scientific trials and the systematic attempts to prove a hypothesis.</note> need only look up my *Experiments*, through which I pave the way to the knowledge of nature and art <note>In the 18th century, "Art" (Kunst) referred to human ingenuity, technology, and mechanics, as opposed to the "Nature" of the created world.</note>: for when I explain the origin of everything that happens, I proceed in exactly the same way as I have indicated here. Whoever, therefore, reads through and ponders these experiments with diligence will, through this practice, achieve proficiency in applying the rules provided.

<margin>Who is most skilled at discovering truths.</margin>

§. 12. It is clear from all that has been said regarding the discovery of truths <term>Warheiten</term> that the more one knows, the more skilled one is at finding something new. For this reason, beginners <term>Anfänger</term> should not seek to act as inventors <term>Erfinder</term> too quickly; rather, they must first learn what others before them have discovered. Nevertheless, it is a very useful exercise to examine the discoveries of others according to the prescribed rules, investigating how they might have been brought to light, if...

...as if they were not yet there. And therefore, one discovery <term>Erfindung</term> <note>In the logic of this era, "Erfindung" refers to both the "invention" of a logical argument and the "discovery" of a scientific fact.</note> always provides an opportunity for another, and we must not regard the great people who lived before us as insignificant simply because they perhaps knew less than we do in our own times. For if we had not had them as predecessors <term>Vorgänger</term>, we would not have surpassed them. I therefore advise those who have a desire to practice their reflection <term>Nachdencken</term> that they should not only examine everything that appears in my *Principles of Mathematical Sciences* <note>original: "Anfangs-Gründe der mathematischen Wiſſenſchaften." This refers to Christian Wolff's foundational textbooks, which were influential across Europe and often published in Latin as *Elementa matheseos universae*.</note>, especially the Latin ones, according to the rules given in these six chapters; they should also apply the same diligence to everything I have written regarding philosophy <term>Welt-Weisheit</term> <note>Literally "world-wisdom," the standard 18th-century German term for philosophy before the Latin-derived "Philosophie" became common.</note>. However, one must not rush, nor worry about one's progress, but rather allow time its way.

<margin>On Corollaries.</margin>
§. 13. Occasionally, beneath an explanation or even a proposition <term>Satz</term>, regardless of its nature, it is customary to note a specific case or to conclude something immediately from it; such truths are called corollaries <term>Zuſätze</term> in mathematics. For example: I have proven that all passions <term>Affecten</term> <note>From the Latin *affectus*, these are intense emotional states—like anger or pride—that Enlightenment thinkers believed could "cloud" the rational mind.</note> hinder a person so that they cannot see the truth <term>Wahrheit</term>, and from this I conclude: Thus, zeal and vengefulness also hinder one from seeing the truth...

Likewise, it is a corollary if I further conclude from this: *Therefore, one must not provoke someone whom one wishes to refute.* In mathematics, where it is customary to assign a name to every type of truth, those things are also counted among the corollaries which could pass for propositions <term>Lehr-Sätze</term> <note>A "Lehr-Satz" (theorem/proposition) is a formal statement that requires a proof, as opposed to an axiom which is self-evident.</note> if their proof is not too extensive and the proposition under which they are placed provides a primary ground for the proof.

On Remarks

§. 14. Specifically, however, they further explain what might still appear obscure <term>dunckel</term> here or there. Such remarks serve greatly in the advancement of the sciences, and it would be desirable for the writings of scholars to be frequently filled with them.

# Chapter 7.

## On Science, Belief, Opinion, and Error.

->§. 1.<-

<margin>The nature of science</margin>
I have already mentioned in the preliminary report (§. 2.) that by science <term>Wissenschafft</term> <note>In the 18th century, "Wissenschafft" (Science) did not just refer to the natural sciences, but to any body of knowledge organized into a rigorous, demonstrable system.</note> I understand a facility of the understanding to prove everything that one asserts, irrefutably, from incontestable grounds. Since definitions, principles, and clear experiences are incontestable grounds (§. 36. ch. 1, §. 13. ch. 3, §. 1. ch. 5), but demonstrations <term>Demonstrationen</term> are those conclusions which are irrefutable (§. 2, 4. ch. 4) and lead far enough until one has nothing in the final conclusion but definitions, clear experiences, or principles as premises <term>Förder-Sätzen</term> <note>Wolff uses "Förder-Sätze" to refer to the premises (propositions that come before) in a syllogism or logical argument.</note> (§. 21. ch. 4): science is therefore nothing other than a facility for demonstrating.

§. 2. Therefore, since in the mathematical disciplines one has until now demonstrated almost exclusively and with precision; the most certain means to attain science is...
