To what 
it 
extends.
</margin>
§. 4. Faith therefore extends only to things that have happened or are to happen. For other matters allow themselves to be proven, and thus one can know them. But that something has happened cannot be proven <note>Wolff distinguishes here between "proving" (logical demonstration from first principles) and "witnessing" (historical evidence). In his system, you cannot "prove" a historical event happened using only logic.</note>. And thus one must trust the testimony of another; that is, believe it.

<margin>
How to avoid credulity.

§. 5. However, to ensure that we are not credulous <note>original: "leichtgläubig"; literally "light-believing" or gullible.</note> and do not allow ourselves to be deceived, we must first satisfy ourselves of two things: 1. That the person who reports something was able to perceive the matter correctly, so that he was not deceived himself; 2. That he recounts the matter exactly as he perceived it, so that he does not have the intention of deceiving others. In a word, I must be certain that the witness is both capable <note>original: "klug"; here implying the mental faculty to observe and understand accurately.</note> and sincere enough.

<margin>
How it is recognized that someone knows the matter correctly.
</margin>
§. 6. The first is recognized by whether the witness was personally present when the event occurred, paid close attention to everything, and possesses as much skill as is required to observe the matter accurately, retain it in his memory, and recount it to others; or, that he received what he recounts from such a witness and is certain that this person had no intention of imposing something false <note>original: "etwas aufzubürden"; literally "to burden him with something," meaning to foist a lie upon him.</note> upon him.

<margin>
Causes that diminish credibility.
</margin>
§. 7. Because, however, anyone can easily change, omit, or add something—as experience sufficiently teaches us—the credibility <note>original: "Glaubwürdigkeit"; the worthiness of being believed.</note> of a testimony also decreases if it has passed through many mouths before we learn of it.

Further such causes.

Chapter 7.

...passes by before we learn of the testimony; for over a long period of time, something can very easily be forgotten.

<margin>Causes why someone intentionally tells something false.</margin>
§. 9. If someone intends to foist a falsehood <note>original: "Unwahrheit aufbürden"; literally to burden someone with an untruth.</note> upon another, there must be a reason <note>original: "raison"; a French loanword common in 18th-century German philosophy, meaning a motivating cause.</note> why he does it; for the will cannot be determined by nothing. Now, it is known from experience that the human will is inclined to do something if it envisions it as good and advantageous; conversely, it is held back from something if it views it as evil and disadvantageous. Therefore, when someone intentionally tells a falsehood, he must do it either because he has an interest <note>original: "interesse"; here meaning a personal stake, benefit, or motivation.</note> in it, or because he believes he is averting some evil by doing so.

<margin>How to investigate the sincerity of a witness.</margin>
§. 10. However, this interest can concern either the soul, the body, the honor, or the external state of fortune, and it can either be a real interest or merely have a false appearance. The same applies to evil. Therefore, one must investigate whether someone could have counted on such an interest in their false account, or whether they feared some evil <note>original: "Ubel"; can mean harm, misfortune, or a bad outcome.</note> if they had been required to tell the truth. It cannot be denied, though, that

<margin>Further elaboration of the previous point.</margin>
§. 11. It is furthermore important to note here that one can hope for good or expect evil either from the persons to whom something is told, or from the persons about whom something is told, or from other persons who are related to both in some way and share a common interest <note>original: "interesse"; in 18th-century logic, this refers to any personal stake, whether financial, social, or emotional.</note> with them. <margin>When a witness is suspicious.</margin>
§. 12. If it is observed that a person could have counted on a real, or even just an apparent, interest resulting from a false account—even if it is not yet clear whether they actually thought about it or not—then one has at least cause to view them with suspicion <note>original: "Verdacht"; a technical term in legal and logical reasoning meaning a reason to withhold full belief.</note>, as to whether they might have allowed some untruth to creep in.

<margin>When the suspicion increases.</margin>
§. 13. If one can conclude from certain circumstances that it is more probable that they did think about [this interest] than that they disregarded <note>original: "regardiret"; a Germanized version of the French "regarder," meaning to consider or pay attention to.</note> it, then the suspicion toward them becomes all the greater.

When a witness is beyond suspicion.

<margin>
How the credibility of the account depends on its circumstances.
</margin>
§. 15. However, the credibility of an account <note>original: "Erzehlung"</note> does not rest solely on the authority of the person who brings it forward, but often also on its own circumstances. For circumstances can sometimes conflict with one another so much that one either clearly sees that the matter being told could not have happened at all, or at least not in the manner claimed; or, at the very least, there are sufficient reasons for a legitimate doubt. Such judgments require a sound intellect and an exact knowledge of the circumstances.

<margin>
Origin of error.
</margin>
§. 16. ...but this is only an **opinion** to them, for they assume: if one separates in thought everything from the **body** <note>original: "Cörper"; referring to physical matter or substance.</note> that belongs to it and is not common to all, then only bare **extension** according to length, breadth, and thickness remains. §. 21. Opinions provide no peace of mind.

§. 22. And in such a way, opinions give no complete **satisfaction** <note>original: "Vergnügung"; refers here to intellectual contentment or the "rest" the mind finds when it is no longer searching for proof.</note> to the **mind** <note>original: "Gemüthe"; the inner seat of thought and feeling.</note>, if one is sensible and thirsts for truth.

# Chapter 8.

## How one should examine one's own powers as well as those of others, to see if they are sufficient for investigating a truth.

->§ 1.<-

<margin>Judging the capacity for reflection.</margin>
Since one can invent <note>original: "erfinden"; in the 18th century, this term referred to both the discovery of scientific truths and the creation of new mechanical devices.</note> nothing out of nothing, but something must always be given from which the other is brought forth, and since this latter process occurs according to certain rules and presupposes a skill <term>skill <note>original: "Fertigkeit"; refers to a developed habit or practiced ability rather than just an innate talent.</note></term> in reflection—as is evident from everything that has been presented here and there in the previous chapters concerning the discovery of truth—one recognizes that our own powers, or those of another, are sufficient to discover or investigate a truth when: 1. everything is well known to us that is presupposed as being necessary to know; 2. we know all the rules necessary for reflection; and 3. we have already frequently practiced our reflection <note>original: "Nachsinnen"; the act of deep, methodical thinking.</note> in similar matters. For example: if the task were given to invent a machine that a certain—

### Chapter 9.

### Section 1.

<margin>How inventions are to be brought into certain classes.</margin>
Since everything we can think of consists of either explanations and descriptions (Section 36, Ch. 1), or <term>propositions <note>Original: "Sätze." In formal logic, these are statements that can be proven true or false.</note></term> (Section 2, Ch. 3), or experiences (Section 1, Ch. 5), and the propositions are either <term>axioms <note>Original: "Grund-Sätze." Fundamental principles accepted without proof.</note></term>, <term>theorems <note>Original: "Lehr-Sätze." Statements that have been logically demonstrated.</note></term>, or <term>problems <note>Original: "Aufgaben." Practical challenges or mathematical tasks requiring a solution.</note></term> (Sections 13 and 14, Ch. 3); therefore, all <term>inventions <note>Original: "Erfindungen." In this period, the term encompassed both mechanical devices and intellectual discoveries or scientific breakthroughs.</note></term> must be divided into these classes. One can then judge without difficulty, based on the definitions given there, into which class an invention belongs (Section 36, Ch. 1). For example: If someone says that <term>heat <note>Original: "Wärme." The author describes a "corpuscular" theory of heat, common in the 17th and 18th centuries, which imagined heat as physical particles in motion.</note></term> is a collection of small particles that have a very pointed shape and are in extremely rapid motion, I can easily see that he intends to explain the nature of heat (Section 48, Ch. 1). If, on the other hand, he says: heat is necessary for human life, then one soon recognizes that

<page-num>160</page-num>
<header>160 Chapter 9. How one should</header>

...it is a <term>theorem <note>Original: "Lehr-Satz." A statement that has been logically proven rather than just observed.</note></term> (§ 14, Ch. 3). Similarly, if I say: **"Honor is a thought regarding the perfection of another,"** I see immediately that this is an <term>explanation <note>Original: "Erklärung." Often used here in the sense of a definition that clarifies the meaning of a concept.</note></term>. On the other hand, if someone says: **"We do not have honor within our own power,"** one quickly recognizes that it is a theorem. But if he says: **"Whoever wishes to honor God must thoroughly recognize His perfections and represent them to himself frequently,"** then it is immediately clear that this is a solution to a <term>problem <note>Original: "Aufgabe." Here referring to a practical challenge or a method of achieving a specific goal.</note></term>.

<margin>When verbal definitions are confused with the explanation of things.</margin>
§ 2. If someone undertakes to explain the essence of a thing, yet relates only some of its properties by which it is distinguished from others of its kind, he confuses <term>verbal definitions <note>Original: "Wort-Erklärungen." Also known as nominal definitions, which describe how a word is used rather than the internal nature of the object itself.</note></term> with the explanation of the things themselves (§ 41, Ch. 1). Thus the **Cartesians** <note>Followers of the French philosopher René Descartes (1596–1650), who sought to ground all knowledge in certain first principles, such as "I think, therefore I am."</note> deceive themselves when they think they have explained the essence of the **soul** by telling us one of its properties and calling it a "thinking being." The same happens to them when they believe they have explained the essence of the **body** by saying it is a being extended in length, width, and thickness. And the **lawyers** are no better off when they believe they understand the nature of a **legal obligation** <note>Original: "Verbindung." In this legal context, it refers to the bond of duty or liability between parties.</note> by saying it is a "bond of law—" <note>Original: "Band des Rechtes." This is a translation of the Latin legal term "vinculum juris," a standard definition of obligation in Roman law.</note>

<summary>This page provides practical examples for distinguishing between definitions, theorems, and problems, while critiquing the Cartesians for offering superficial "verbal" definitions rather than explaining the true essence of the soul and body.</summary>

<page-num>161</page-num>
<header>...recognizing one’s own or foreign inventions.</header>

...ties, by which one is urged to do or to refrain from doing something.

<margin>Those who cannot distinguish between words and things.</margin>
§ 3. Those who perceive the **effects** of things and name that from which they originate with a specific name, and then cite this name in place of the cause of the effect, are unable to distinguish between words and things. Because they can think of nothing when using the word except the effect whose cause they desire to know—and thus have no concept of this cause at all (§ 4, Ch. 1)—their word is merely an **empty sound** (§ 3, Ch. 2). Since such a person believes he is stating the cause when he merely names a word, he must necessarily be unable to differentiate between words and things. For example: the **Schoolmen** <note>Original: "Schul-Weisen." This refers to the Scholastic philosophers, the traditional academics of the Middle Ages and early modern period who followed the teachings of Aristotle and the Church Fathers.</note> observed many effects in plants: that they nourished themselves, that they grew, and that they reproduced their own kind. Since they did not understand the **anatomy of plants**, they named that from which these effects originated a **vegetative soul** <term>vegetative soul <note>Original: "Animam vegetativam." In traditional philosophy, this was thought to be the lowest form of life-force that directed the biological functions of plants, such as growth and nutrition, without involving sensation or thought.</note></term>. For every different effect they encountered in plants, they attributed a corresponding **power** to this soul, such as a power to draw sap through the roots, or a power [to transport] the same through...

<meta>catchword: the</meta>
<meta>(Logic) §</meta>

<summary>This page critiques the "Schoolmen" for using empty terminology, like the "vegetative soul," to name effects without actually understanding the underlying physical causes or plant anatomy. It argues that mistaking a name for an explanation is a failure to distinguish between mere words and actual things.</summary>
<keywords>Scholasticism, cause and effect, vegetative soul, plant anatomy, nominal definitions, philosophical errors, logic</keywords>

How to

§ 5. When someone explains a matter and assumes such things whose possibility is not assured, then his explanation is **uncertain**. If he assumes impossible things, then it is **false**. For we call that **uncertain** of which we are not yet fully convinced whether it be possible or not; we call it **false** when we say that something could be which is, in fact, impossible. E.g. <note>Original: "Z. E." for "Zum Exempel."</note> If some wish to explain the **nature of water**, they assign a **cylindrical shape** to its particles <note>A reference to "mechanical philosophy," where 17th-century thinkers like Descartes tried to explain the properties of matter (like the flow of water) through the physical shape of its microscopic parts.</note>. They assume this type of particle but cannot prove that they are to be found in water. Therefore, their explanation is **uncertain** and merely a **conjecture**. When the ancients <note>Referring to the classical medical tradition of Hippocrates and Galen.</note> were tasked to explain the **digestion of food**, they assumed such a great **heat** in the stomach that it could boil everything down completely <note>The ancient theory of "coction," which viewed digestion as a form of internal cooking.</note>. However, **experience** <note>Meaning empirical observation or experimental evidence.</note> shows that it is impossible to find such heat in the stomach. Therefore, this explanation is **false**. If all things are not cited which contribute to the formation of a matter, or if that which they contribute is not presented in sufficient detail, then the explanation is...

<margin>How theorems and fundamental principles are mixed together.</margin>
§. 7. Sometimes <term>theorems<gloss>Lehr-Sätze; propositions that require a logical proof to be accepted as true</gloss></term> are presented as <term>axioms<gloss>Grund-Sätze; fundamental principles that are self-evidently true and require no proof</gloss></term>, which one can recognize very quickly if one only pays attention to whether they are clear from a single definition or not (§. 13. ch. 3), or whether one finds many words within them instead, from whose distinct concepts a proof must be derived. Now, when it is necessary for a proposition to be proven, it is not rightly presented as an axiom and accepted without proof. Therefore, in such cases, one judges that a person accepts propositions unproven because they cannot prove them, even though they ought to be proven. This is how it is commonly done by those who wish to demonstrate in a mathematical manner outside of mathematics <note>The author is likely critiquing 18th-century "Rationalist" philosophers who tried to apply the rigid structure of geometry to subjects like ethics or theology without providing the same level of self-evident foundations.</note>.

<margin>
Cases regarding judgments of tasks.
</margin>

§. 9. In the solution of a <term>task<gloss>Aufgabe; in 18th-century logic, a "problem" or "task" is a proposition that requires an action or a construction, rather than just a statement of fact</gloss></term>, it is shown how something can be done or made, or also how it can happen (§. 14. c. 3). Consequently, the following cases occur here. One looks either at the things themselves or at their correctness. Regarding the first, that which is prescribed is either sufficient or it is not; or it does not serve the matter at all—indeed, it may even be a hindrance. Regarding the second, it is clear from experience or through proof that either everything prescribed is possible, or that it is not possible, or it is still unknown whether it is possible or not; and even if it is possible, the manner and method of how to <term>put it into practice<gloss>ins Werck zu stellen; to implement, execute, or physically construct</gloss></term> is not known. Thus, many judgments arise from the solutions of tasks.

<margin>
When a solution is correct and incorrect.
</margin>

§. 10. When one is convinced through experience or also through a <term>demonstration<gloss>original: "Demonstration"; a rigorous, mathematical-style proof that leaves no room for doubt</gloss></term> that if only that which is specified in the solution of the task is performed, then that which the task requires must also follow; furthermore, if it is no less established—either through experience or through prior proofs—that everything prescribed is possible, and additionally the way to put everything into practice is known: then the solution is true, complete, and accurate,

And for that reason, the solution to the task is false.

§. 17. Furthermore, one must take care not to immediately declare something as generally uncertain simply because it is uncertain to us. For we cannot assert such a thing until we are able to demonstrate that another person is just as unable as we are to determine the correctness of the **premises** <term>premises<gloss>original: "Vorder-Sätze"; the foundational statements in a logical argument from which a conclusion is drawn.</gloss></term> from which the proposition (which is uncertain to us) was concluded; or, if it concerns a concept, that the path to it is blocked for another just as much as for ourselves. But unfortunately, nothing is more common than judging others by our own standards in general, and especially in this particular matter.

<margin>
How to judge utility.

§. 18. Above all, however, we must
take care when we wish

**knowledge** <gloss>original: "Erkäntnis"</gloss> is called useful when it
promotes the **convenience** <note>original: "Bequemlichkeit"; in the 18th century, this referred to the ease, well-being, or general suitability of human life rather than just physical comfort.</note> of human life;
and since this must be judged partly
from the perfection of the **soul** <gloss>original: "Seele"</gloss>,
partly from the perfection of the **body** <gloss>original: "Leib"</gloss>,
partly from the perfection of the external

preserved, or more easily attained, or

so one cannot call a piece of knowledge
useless until one can demonstrate that
it is impossible for a case to occur in
which it contributes, either indirectly
or directly, to one of the aforementioned
perfections. This proof presupposes
great knowledge and is therefore
difficult to undertake.
In all other cases, we can only
say that the utility is unknown
to us, or perhaps we are unable,
or do not know how to
make use of it.

<meta>catchword: The</meta>

<summary>This page argues that one should be slow to dismiss any knowledge as useless, because "utility" is broadly defined as anything that improves the soul, body, or life's circumstances. The author notes that proving something is truly useless requires nearly impossible levels of total knowledge.</summary>

<header>Chapter 10. How one</header>

## Chapter 10.

# How one should judge Writings.

->§. 1.<-

<margin>Classification of writings.</margin>Writings deal either with **histories** <term>histories<gloss>original: "Geschichten"; in this period, the term encompasses both stories and the factual recording of events.</gloss></term>, or they present certain **doctrines**. The first type of writings, which are usually called **historical**, recount either what has happened in **nature** <note>This refers to "Natural History," which in the 18th century included the study of animals, plants, and minerals.</note>, or what has occurred among **human beings**.

<margin>Virtues of historical writings.</margin>§. 2. One can therefore require no more from a historical writing than that everything be recounted in such **order** and with such **circumstances** as it actually happened. Therefore, there are three virtues of historical writings: **truth**, **completeness**, and **order**.

<margin>How to judge their truth.</margin>§. 3. # Chapter 10: How one should judge historical writings

...common good <note>original: "Bestens"; likely completing "gemeinen Bestens" from the previous page, referring to the collective welfare of society.</note> cannot be omitted, as both are indissolubly linked with it: of which one can gain a clearer understanding from my *Morals* <note>Referring to the author's work on ethics, likely Christian Wolff’s *German Ethics* (Vernünfftige Gedancken von der Menschen Thun und Lassen).</note>. This serves to better understand what I have written in my *Morals* concerning duties toward God and in my *Metaphysics* concerning the attributes of God.

Those [grounds or premises] are either obviously true, or false, or still doubtful. Again, either the following propositions are consistently concluded from the preceding ones, or each proposition has its own particular grounds from which it is proven.

<margin>Caution to be exercised in this matter.</margin>
§. 13. <margin>How one errs in this judgment.</margin>
§. 20. In general, those who lack understanding <term>understanding<gloss>original: "Unverständigen"</gloss></term> form completely different concepts of obscurity <term>obscurity<gloss>original: "Dunckelheit"</gloss></term> and clarity <term>clarity<gloss>original: "Deutlichkeit"</gloss></term> <note>In the philosophy of this period, "clarity" and "distinctness" were technical terms for how well a concept could be recognized and its individual parts identified.</note>. Specifically, they call "obscure" that which requires more reflection than they have previously applied to the things they have learned; on the other hand, they call "clear" that which does not require additional reflection. Rather, they should say: "it is difficult for me," or "it is easy for me," because I am unaccustomed or, conversely, accustomed to such things. Out of this prejudice, many call mathematical writings and all thoroughly established truths "obscure."

<margin>When everything is well-connected.</margin>
§. 21. Finally, if the subsequent propositions <term>propositions<gloss>original: "Sätze"</gloss></term> are consistently derived from the preceding ones, as is customary in the mathematical sciences, then everything is well-connected <term>well-connected<gloss>original: "wohl an einander hänget"</gloss></term>. Otherwise, the matters do not hang together.

<margin>Prejudices regarding order</margin>
§. 22. <header>Chapter 10. How one</header>

...sets things in order with one another, and how one may most aptly be proven from the other. The first is the **School-Order** <term>School-Order<gloss>original: "Schul-Ordnung"</gloss></term> and has been favored by common scholars; the other, however, is the **Order of Nature**, which pleases the **Mathematicians** <term>mathematicians<gloss>original: "Mathematicis"</gloss></term> best. Following the former, one cannot prove everything precisely, as the ***Elements of Geometry*** <term>Elements of Geometry<gloss>original: "Elmens de Geometrie"</gloss></term> by **Arnauld** and **Lamy** <note>Antoine Arnauld and Bernard Lamy were influential 17th-century French thinkers who sought to make geometry easier to learn by reorganizing Euclid’s traditional structure.</note> demonstrate; following the latter, one must yield nothing in the proof, as the writings of the ancient **Geometers** <term>geometers<gloss>original: "Geometrarum"</gloss></term> confirm. Therefore, one leaves the School-Order to common scholars and beginners; but those who intend to see deeper into the truth choose the Order of Nature. And I follow this latter order as well, both in my mathematical writings and in the others I have published on **Philosophy** <term>philosophy<gloss>original: "Welt-Weiſheit"</gloss></term> <note>The author uses "Welt-Weiſheit" (world-wisdom), which was the standard 18th-century German term for philosophy.</note>.

### Chapter II.

# How One Should Rightly Read Books with Profit.

->§. 1.<-

<margin>The intention of those who read.</margin>
We read books so that we may learn to recognize what is contained within them. Thus, two things are required: 1. that we rightly understand the author <term>author<gloss>original: "Urheber"</gloss></term>, and 2. that we grasp the subjects presented well.

<header>Chapter 11. How one</header>

...and free one’s mind <term>mind<gloss>original: "Gemüthe"</gloss></term> from extraneous thoughts. If one wishes to grasp it well, one must not read too quickly, nor too much at once (§. 24. c). <margin>How one should take heed of the intention of the book and its parts.</margin>
§. 3. ## Chapter 13.
# How to convince someone.

### §. 1.
<margin>
What it means to convince.
</margin>
Whoever seeks to **convince** <term>convince<gloss>original: "überführen"; in this context, to provide a logical demonstration that compels the mind to accept a truth.</gloss></term> someone intends to make them certain that something is true or false, probable or improbable. For example: That in the **proposition** <term>proposition<gloss>original: "Satze"</gloss></term>, "the moon is a light," the **predicate** <term>predicate<gloss>original: "Hinter-Glied"; literally "back-member" or "consequent."</gloss></term> "light" is correctly connected to the **subject** <term>subject<gloss>original: "Förder-Glied"; literally "front-member" or "antecedent."</gloss></term> "the moon."

### §. 2.
<margin>
How the words must be explained.
</margin>
Therefore, before all else, every word found in a proposition must be explained so that the person one wishes to convince understands it (§. 2. ch. 2); unless one is sufficiently assured that they already possess all the necessary concepts beforehand. As in the previous proposition, it is not necessary for me to explain what the **moon** is, because everyone knows that by this we mean the celestial body which, among all the stars of heaven, illuminates the earth most during the nighttime. However, I can indeed explain what **light** is, namely that which [makes] the bodies lying around us visi-

Therefore, we can convince another person in no other way. ...[thin]gs, which are accepted for forming the others, must be established either through experience or through proof (§ 49, ch. 1).

<margin>How it is with experiences.</margin>
§ 9. When one is dealing with experiences <term>experiences<gloss>original: "Erfahrungen"; empirical observations or experimental data.</gloss></term>, and it is not in our power to bring the other person to them—such as when one speaks of the corona <note>original: "Krone"; the solar corona visible during a total solar eclipse, which at the time was sometimes discussed in relation to the moon's atmosphere.</note> around the moon in solar eclipses, or the altered shape of the fixed stars as they come close to the moon, which the famous astronomer <term>astronomer<gloss>Latin original: "Astronomus"</gloss></term> Cassini <note>Giovanni Domenico Cassini (1625–1712), a prominent astronomer at the Paris Observatory.</note> observed—then one must only appeal to them and cite everything that serves their credibility <term>credibility<gloss>original: "Glaubwürdigkeit"; the reliability or trustworthiness of a report or witness.</gloss></term> (§ 5 & seqq. ch. 7). In this case, then, the conviction <term>conviction<gloss>original: "Überführung"; the state of being logically persuaded or "overcome" by evidence.</gloss></term> is as great as the degrees of credibility allow.

<margin>What is required of the one who is to be convinced.</margin>
§ 10. In particular, he who is to be convinced must pay close attention to everything said to him; and if he is not yet practiced in such matters, he must reflect upon each point several times according to the rules I gave in the 9th chapter. To this reflection, the other person must provide all necessary assistance. One must also have time for this, if one is not yet well-versed <note>original: "gewieget"; literally "cradled," meaning to be experienced or "at home" in a subject.</note> in a matter, and therefore, like a beginner, go through something every day and diligently repeat on the following days what was learned on the previous ones, so that one becomes familiar with the subject matter.

# Chapter 13.

<margin>
Those who cannot be convinced through their own fault.
</margin>
§ 11. Now, if someone is far too impatient or too hasty to pay attention to a matter and reflect upon it in the prescribed manner, or to allow time to take its course, then one strives in vain to convince <term>convince<gloss>original: "überführen"; in a logical sense, to bring someone to an inescapable conclusion through evidence.</gloss></term> them. In such a case, the fault lies with that person that they are not convinced.

<margin>
Those who cannot be convinced through our fault.
</margin>
§ 12. However, the fault can also be on our side: specifically, if we assume things to be possible or impossible whose possibility or impossibility has not yet been recognized by the other person whom we intend to convince; or if we do not explain our words clearly enough to them (§ 2, 3). <note>The author emphasizes that communication fails when the speaker assumes the listener shares their foundational premises or understands their vocabulary without proper definitions.</note>

<margin>
How one considers oneself convinced without grounds.
</margin>
§ 13. One must take care not to mistake persuasion <term>persuasion<gloss>original: "Uberredung"; a belief based on rhetoric or emotion rather than logical proof.</gloss></term>—that is, a false delusion <term>delusion<gloss>original: "Wahn"; an illusory belief or unfounded opinion.</gloss></term> as if we recognized the correctness of the matter when we are still far from seeing it—for a true conviction. Such a delusion arises, however: 1. when we are not practiced in reflection <term>reflection<gloss>original: "Nachdencken"; the active mental process of weighing evidence.</gloss></term> and do not understand what is required for a complete conviction; 2. when we hold prejudices regarding certain people, as if their intellect were so sharp-witted <term>sharp-witted<gloss>original: "scharffsinnig"; possessing keen analytical ability.</gloss></term> that they could bring forth nothing incorrect, and therefore we hold something as true simply because they said it, even if the cited reasons val- <note>original catchword: "gel-"; likely leading to "gelten" (to be valid) or "gelehrt" (learned) on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page explores why logical conviction fails, attributing it to either the listener's impatience or the speaker's lack of clarity, while warning against mistaking mere persuasion and authority-based prejudice for true understanding.</summary>

<margin>
Remedy to correct the first error.

§ 14. The first error is corrected, partly when one diligently practices mathematics—in which we can practice reflection <term>reflection<gloss>original: "Nachdencken"; the active, systematic process of thinking through a problem.</gloss></term> as much as we like and always reach a successful conclusion because we encounter demonstrated truths there—and partly when one becomes familiar with the rules necessary for reflection, of which a good supply can be found in my present work. <note>The author promotes mathematics not just for its own sake, but as a "gymnasium for the mind" to build the stamina needed for logical thought.</note>

<margin>
Remedy against the second error.
</margin>
§ 15. To prevent the second error, by which the studying youth especially are blinded (even when they constantly speak against such blindness with their mouths), it would certainly be very useful if, above all else (§ 14), the first error were corrected. However, because those whose interest it is to keep others in blindness through their own authority often hinder this, one must simply show through obvious examples how great men—and even more often those who only consider and present themselves as great—have failed. This is so that our confidence in their ability [does not keep us] from proper investigation...

§ 16. <margin>How one refutes another.</margin>
§ 4. <margin>How to take care with one’s words.</margin>
§ 5. Now, since the person who is to be convinced must pay close attention to everything (§ 10, ch. 13), one must abstain from everything that can incense our opponent <term>incense our opponent<gloss>original: "in den Harnisch jagen"; literally "to drive into the harness," meaning to provoke someone to fly into a passion or prepare for a fight.</gloss></term>. In such a way, one must speak and write so that he can draw no suspicion from it that one holds him in low regard or despises him—especially if he has merits of his own. And, in general, nothing must shine forth from our refutation but sincerity, a love for the truth, and a love for the one who errs; but by no means envy, ambition, or a desire to harm the opponent. In a word: virtue, and no vice.

<margin>The origin of abusive and insulting words in refutations.</margin>
§ 6. Whoever proceeds otherwise behaves either as a fool or as a person of vice. ## Chapter 15.
# How one should conduct a debate.

->§. 1.<-

<margin>What disputation is.</margin>
Whoever debates with another has set out to orally convince him of the error of the propositions <note>original: "Sätze"; in this context, it refers to the formal logical theses or statements being defended.</note> that he asserts.

->§. 2.<-

<margin>The debaters must contradict one another.</margin>
Therefore, he must deny what the other asserts; assert what the other denies; present as certain what the other holds only as probable; and hold as uncertain what seems certain to the other. In such a manner, the debaters <term>disputants<gloss>original: "Disputanten"; the participants in a formal, rule-bound academic argument.</gloss></term> must always contradict one another in some way.

->§. 3.<-

<margin>How word-strife is to be avoided.</margin>
For this reason, it is necessary above all things that the words appearing in the proposition be explained, so that it does not result in a mere word-strife <note>original: "Wort-Streit"; a disagreement over the definitions of words rather than the underlying facts or logic.</note> (§. 2. c. 2). And therefore, when the meaning is somewhat obscure, one is accustomed to ask in what sense they take this or that word.

->§. 4.<-

<margin>How to bring the respondent to explain his [meaning]</margin>
Sometimes the respondent <term>respondent<gloss>original: "Respondente"; the person who defends the thesis and answers the objections of the opponent.</gloss></term> refuses to provide an explanation because, due to a lack of understanding, he imagines that one only wishes to vex <note>original: "vexiren"; to tease, annoy, or harass, implying the opponent is being pedantic just to be difficult.</note> him, or perhaps he lacks a clear concept of his own words, and thus [cannot]

<note>The vocabulary list below clarifies the technical terms used in early modern logic and debate.</note>
debate, error, propositions, debaters, word-strife, respondent, explanation, vex

Chapter 15.
<meta>catchword: §. 6.</meta>

<margin>...to provide an explanation.</margin>...cannot explain. In order to force an explanation from him, we must accuse his proposition of <term>obscurity<gloss>original: "Dunckelheit"; a lack of clarity in language that prevents understanding.</gloss></term>, on the grounds that the words contained therein have no correct meaning. For if he asserts that they do have a correct meaning, he must explain them, as the meaning of words is arbitrary <note>The author refers to the philosophical idea that words are human conventions rather than natural laws; therefore, one person cannot "guess" the specific definition another person is using without a shared explanation.</note> and thus the other person cannot guess it.

<margin>What the opponent has to do.</margin>
§. 5. Once the parties are in agreement regarding the meaning, the <term>opponent<gloss>original: "Opponente"; the person whose role is to challenge the thesis.</gloss></term> begins to present his proof and continues it until his conclusions lead back to <term>premises<gloss>original: "Förder-Sätze"; literally "advancement-propositions," these are the preliminary statements or assumptions upon which a conclusion is built.</gloss></term> that are known to the respondent and which he accepts (§. 21. c. 4). However, since the respondent must recognize both the correctness of the premises within the arguments and the validity of the arguments themselves (§. 1. c. 13), the proof must be structured into formal <term>syllogisms<gloss>original: "Schlüsse"; logical arguments where a conclusion is drawn from two or more premises.</gloss></term>. Occasionally, one may omit a premise and thus make use of a <term>truncated syllogism<gloss>original: "verstümmelten Schlusses"; known in formal logic as an enthymeme, where part of the argument is implied rather than stated.</gloss></term> (§. 17. c. 4), provided one is certain that the respondent will not deny it. The process must begin, however, with the final conclusion, the <term>consequent<gloss>original: "Hinter-Satz"; the final statement of a syllogism that follows from the premises.</gloss></term> of which contradicts the respondent’s original proposition.

# How one should conduct a debate.

<margin>What the respondent has to do.</margin>
§. 6. The <term>Respondent<gloss>original: "Respondente"; the person, usually a student, whose role is to defend a specific thesis against challenges.</gloss></term> must accept all <term>syllogisms<gloss>original: "Schlüsse"; formal logical arguments consisting of premises and a conclusion.</gloss></term> (§. 10. c. 13), and to demonstrate that he has understood them correctly, he must repeat them once more. If the syllogism is incorrect in its logical form, he must point this out; however, if the form is correct, he must either grant the conclusion entirely or deny one—and occasionally both—<term>premises<gloss>original: "Förder-Sätze"; literally "advancing-propositions," these are the statements that lead toward the conclusion.</gloss></term>, doing so either unconditionally or under certain conditions.

<margin>What the opponent has to do further.</margin>
§. 7. The premise that the respondent denies must then be proven by the <term>Opponent<gloss>original: "Opponente"; the person tasked with finding logical flaws in the respondent's thesis.</gloss></term> using a new syllogism, in which that denied premise becomes the <term>conclusion<gloss>original: "Hinter-Satz"; literally the "rear-proposition" or the final statement resulting from the logic.</gloss></term>. Furthermore, if the respondent applies a <term>limitation<gloss>original: "limitation"; a qualification or specific condition that restricts the scope of a statement.</gloss></term>, the opponent must either accept that qualification or demonstrate its incorrectness (§. 5).

<margin>Why one disputes through formal syllogisms.</margin>
§. 8. Anyone who reflects upon what was said above regarding demonstrations and confirmed through an example from <term>geometry<gloss>The branch of mathematics dealing with points, lines, and shapes; in this period, it was viewed as the ultimate model for perfect, indisputable logical proof.</gloss></term> (§. 23. 25. c. 4) will not find it strange that we require nothing but formal syllogisms in debating and reject the <term>casual discourses<gloss>original: "Discurse"; informal, conversational speech that lacks strict logical structure.</gloss></term> common among us. For as soon as the debaters begin to speak informally, they generally stray from the subject and never reach a resolution.

<margin>What the Presider has to do.</margin>
§. 9. Because the <term>Presider<gloss>original: "Præses"; the professor or moderator who oversees the disputation and ensures rules are followed.</gloss></term> takes the place of the respondent if the latter is no longer able to proceed—but not, as some <unclear>imagine</unclear> <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "ein=" suggests the next word is "einbilden" (to imagine/fancy).</note>

### Chapter 15. How one should conduct a debate.

->They both stand as one man.<-
<note>This phrase suggests that the Respondent and the Opponent, despite their conflicting roles, are part of a unified academic exercise governed by shared rules of decorum.</note>

§. 10. What formula the <term>Respondent<gloss>original: "Respondente"; the person defending the thesis.</gloss></term> should use when inviting the <term>Opponent<gloss>original: "Opponente"; the person challenging the thesis.</gloss></term>, and what the Opponent should say when expressing thanks; we leave to established custom, which can best teach such matters. <note>Formal academic debates in the early modern period were highly ritualized, beginning with specific Latin greetings and invitations.</note>

<margin>How to debate through questions.</margin>
§. 11. Some are of the opinion that it would be better to debate through questions: however, whoever wishes to debate skillfully through questions must first be able to debate well through <term>syllogisms<gloss>original: "Schlüsse"; formal logical arguments.</gloss></term>. For everything here depends on whether we ask if the other party grants us these or those <term>premises<gloss>original: "Förder-Sätze"; the propositions that lead toward a conclusion.</gloss></term>, or demands that they be further proven; likewise, whether he grants the <term>conclusion<gloss>original: "Hinter-Satz"; the final statement resulting from the premises.</gloss></term> for the sake of those premises.

<meta>This page continues the first index of logical and philosophical terminology, picking up from the catchword "Behind-" (Hinter-) on the previous page. It provides a standardized vocabulary for the components of syllogisms and the different ways concepts can be categorized or analyzed.</meta>

## The First Index.
---

Conclusion <note>original: "Hinter-Satz"; Latin: "conclusio"; the final statement in a logical argument that follows from the premises</note>.
Clear concept <note>original: "Klarer Begriff"; Latin: "notio clara"</note>.
Theorem <note>original: "Lehr-Satz"; Latin: "theorema"; a proposition that is to be demonstrated or proven</note>.
Middle term <note>original: "Mittel-Glied"; Latin: "medius terminus"; the concept that links the two premises of a syllogism but does not appear in the final conclusion</note>.
Major premise <note>original: "Ober-Satz"; Latin: "major scilicet propositio"; the premise containing the major term, usually stated first</note>.
Real definition <note>original: "Sach-Erklärung"; Latin: "definitio realis"; a definition that explains the essential nature of a thing rather than just how the word is used</note>.
Proposition <note>original: "Satz"; Latin: "propositio"; a statement or assertion</note>.
To conceive of a proposition <note>original: "Einen Satz gedencken"; Latin: "propositionem concipere"</note>.
To be unable to conceive [of a proposition] <note>original: "nicht können gedencken"; Latin: "non posse concipere"</note>.
To not know whether it can be conceived or not <note>original: "nicht wissen, ob er sich gedencken lasse, oder nicht"; Latin: "nullum ejus habere conceptum"</note>.
Syllogism <note>original: "Schluß"; Latin: "Syllogismus"; a formal logical argument consisting of three parts: a major premise, a minor premise, and a conclusion</note>.
Hypothetical syllogism <note>original: "Schluß unter einer Bedingung"; Latin: "syllogismus hypotheticus"; an argument based on a "if... then..." condition</note>.
Connection of concepts <note>original: "Verknüpfung der Begriffe"; Latin: "nexus notionum"</note>.
Negative proposition <note>original: "Verneinender Satz"; Latin: "propositio negativa"</note>.
Enthymeme <note>original: "Verstümmelter Schluß"; Latin: "enthymema"; a "mutilated" or truncated syllogism in which one of the premises is left unstated</note>.
Cryptic syllogism <note>original: "Versteckter Schluß"; Latin: "Syllogismus crypticus"; a syllogism where the logical structure is hidden or not immediately obvious</note>.
Incomplete concept <note>original: "Unausführlicher Begriff"; Latin: "notio incompleta"</note>.
Confused concept <note>original: "Undeutlicher Begriff"; Latin: "notio confusa"</note>.
Immediate consequence <note>original: "Unmittelbahre Folge"; Latin: "consequentia immediata"</note>.
Minor premise <note>original: "Unter-Satz"; Latin: "minor scilicet propositio"; the premise containing the subject of the conclusion</note>.
Inadequate concept <note>original: "Unvollständiger Begriff"; Latin: "notio inadæquata"</note>.
Adequate concept <note>original: "Vollständiger Begriff"; Latin: "notio adæquata"</note>.
Arbitrary concept <note>original: "Willkührlich formirter Begriff"; Latin: "notio arbitraria"</note>.
Nominal definition <note>original: "Wort-Erklärung"; Latin: "definitio nominalis"; a definition that explains the meaning of a word rather than the essence of the thing</note>.
Dispute over words <note>original: "Wort-Streit"; Latin: "logomachia"; a disagreement that arises from different uses of words rather than a real disagreement on facts</note>.
Analysis of concepts <note>original: "Zergliederung der Begriffe"; Latin: "analysis notionum"</note>.
Analysis of the proposition <note>original: "des Satzes"; Latin: "analysis propositionis"</note>.
Corollary <note>original: "Zusatz"; Latin: "corollarium"; an additional deduction that follows directly from a previously proven statement</note>.

<note>The original text includes the following vocabulary summary: Logic, Syllogism, Proposition, Concept, Theorem, Analysis, Index.</note>

<summary>This page serves as a glossary of logical terms, providing German and Latin equivalents for the components of syllogisms (premises, middle terms, conclusions) and different classifications of concepts.</summary>
<keywords>logic, syllogism, major premise, minor premise, real definition, nominal definition, enthymeme, concepts, analysis, corollary</keywords>

-># The Second Index.<-

...have none, 64. They are mixed with things, 161. How their meaning in Scripture <note>original: "Schrifft" (Holy Scripture/The Bible).</note> is to be found, 188 and following. <note>original: "& seqq." (et sequentes), meaning "and the following pages."</note>
**Word-Explanation.** <note>original: "Wort-Erklärung." This refers to what philosophers call a "nominal definition"—explaining the use of a term rather than the essential nature of the object it describes.</note> Its nature, 47. How they are found, 47. Errors of the same, 48 and following. Their utility, 50. Who mixes them with the explanation of the thing, 160.
**Verbal Disputes.** <note>original: "Wort-Streite." These are "logomachies" or arguments where the participants are actually in agreement about the facts but are using different definitions for their terms.</note> Whence they come, 65. How they are to be avoided, 202, 203.
**Numbers.** How the problems thereof are solved, 129.
**Analysis** <note>original: "Zergliederung"; literally "dismemberment" or "dissection." In logic, this is the process of breaking down complex ideas into their simplest parts.</note> of concepts, 27. Of propositions, 71, 72. Utility of the same, 73.
**Witness.** How he is to be examined, whether he is suspicious or not, 144 and following.
**Additions.** <note>original: "Zusätze." In the mathematical-logical style of the German Enlightenment, these are often "corollaries"—propositions that follow naturally from a previously proven statement.</note> What they are, 139, 140.
**Digons.** <note>original: "Zwey-Ecke"; literally "two-corners." A geometric figure with two angles. The text observes that a rectilinear (straight-lined) digon is impossible in Euclidean geometry.</note> A rectilinear one is impossible, 42, 61.
